#2868. Ugly wins count, too. And the Suns will take every one.

You know what I like? When the Phoenix Suns win basketball games. Sometimes it's not pretty, and sometimes the journey to the final buzzer is far more stressful than it needs to be. But as long as the Suns walk away with a victory, there's reason to feel positive. Following Thursday night's win over the Charlotte Hornets, that's exactly how I'm feeling.

Phoenix has now won two consecutive games and sits one victory shy of the .500 mark. Once again, the bench unit provided the spark, serving as the catalyst that helped propel the Suns to a 17-point advantage. For a stretch, it appeared Phoenix was on its way to a relatively comfortable victory, the kind where you could sit back, relax, and enjoy the basketball without wondering how the team might find a way to complicate things.

But this is the Phoenix Suns we're talking about. That 17-point lead? Evaporated.

The Suns were outrebounded 52-31, continuing a troubling trend that has plagued them throughout the early portion of the season. And quite honestly, had Charlotte been even remotely competent at taking care of the basketball, Phoenix might have messed around and lost this one. The Hornets committed a staggering 27 turnovers, and while it's tempting to look at that number and credit the Suns' defensive pressure, the reality is a little more complicated. Phoenix finished with 11 steals, certainly demonstrating its ability to disrupt passing lanes and create opportunities in transition. But a significant portion of Charlotte's turnovers came from careless passes and poor decision-making, the kind of mistakes that reveal a team struggling with connectivity and offensive cohesion.

Still, Phoenix deserves credit for capitalizing on those mistakes. The Suns generated 38 points off turnovers, a massive contribution that ultimately played a significant role in securing the victory. They also took care of the basketball themselves, committing only eight turnovers, which provided a welcome example of offensive discipline.

There were other positives as well. Despite being thoroughly dominated on the glass, Phoenix matched Charlotte with 38 points in the paint. And while the Hornets held a 16-7 advantage in second-chance points, the damage could have been considerably worse given the disparity in rebounding. That doesn't excuse the ongoing problems on the boards, but it does provide some perspective on how the Suns managed to survive another evening in which securing missed shots appeared to be an optional activity.

The rebounding remains a major concern, and there are certainly other issues worth examining over the next couple of days before the Suns welcome the Los Angeles Lakers to town on Sunday. We'll have plenty of time to dissect what went wrong, what needs to improve, and why this team continues to make certain aspects of the game far more difficult than necessary.

But there were encouraging moments, too. The bench continues to produce, the Suns are generating points off turnovers, and they're beginning to string together victories despite their imperfections. And ultimately, the most important statistic is the one that matters more than anything else. The Phoenix Suns won the basketball game. I'll take it.

Goodie sparked the offense against the Clippers, and his 18 points in 13 minutes made him the easiest Bright Side Baller selection of the season.

#2867. Eagles will release WR Hollywood Brown, at his request

Receiver Hollywood Brown, who was available in trade but had no takers, will now be released at his request, via Tim McManus of ESPN.

Thanks to a "noninjury settlement" (which presumably includes Brown waiving his right to termination pay), Brown will be released. Because the trade deadline has passed, Brown's contract will be subject to waivers.

Brown signed a one-year deal with a $3.7 million signing bonus and a $1.3 million base salary. In addition to waiving his right to termination pay, Brown possibly agreed to return a portion of the bonus money.

A first-round pick of the Ravens in 2019, Brown appeared in only four games for the Eagles, with one start. He caught five passes for 63 yards.

Brown spent three years with the Ravens. In 2022, he was traded to the Cardinals. After two seasons in Arizona, he spent 2024 and 2025 with the Chiefs.

The minimum base salary could prompt another team to make a waivers claim — especially if that team would be willing to pay more than the prorated minimum to add him to the roster for the rest of the season.

Brown's best year came in 2021, with 91 catches for 1,008 yards and six touchdowns.

#2866. NHL’s next TV deal will test the league’s strength while media companies prepare for NFL rights increase

One of the biggest developing stories in entertainment and sports is how the world's biggest media companies judge the value of non-NFL rights. The NHL will be the first public test into this new era of media valuation.

The NHL can begin formally negotiating a new media rights deal with its incumbent partners — Disney/ESPN and Skydance, the new owner of TNT Sports — on Jan. 1. The league wants an increase in overall rights fees between 50% and 100% over its current deal, according to sources familiar with the matter. The NHL inked a 7-year, $4.5 billion deal with ESPN and TNT Sports in 2021.

Whether the league actually gets that type of increase is still unclear — and that will be one of the most watched data points in the world of sports business next year.

National media rights for the NHL currently make up about 13% of its annual revenue, which is around $8 billion, according to CNBC estimates.

Disney/ESPN and Skydance want to renew a deal with the league, according to people familiar with the matter that asked not to be named because the discussions are private. Both companies have engaged in preliminary conversations with the league in the past few weeks, and while there's still work to do in terms of price, there's mutual interest in getting something done.

In its current deal, ESPN has access to 100 exclusive games for the 2026-27 season. Fifty-three of them air on ESPN or ABC, and the other 47 exclusive stream on ESPN. Under current terms, the league's playoffs are split between Disney and TNT Sports.

Disney is particularly interested in acquiring the rights to more playoff games, one of the people said. The NHL had its most-watched playoffs on record last season, averaging 1.8 million viewers per game. The Stanley Cup averaged 5.2 million viewers — the largest audience since 2019. It was played between the Carolina Hurricanes and the Vegas Golden Knights — not exactly marquee NHL franchises.

Last year's regular season averaged 546,000 viewers across ESPN/ABC and TNT Sports, up 25% from a year ago — the highest average in more than a decade.

The sport is also having a moment culturally. Earlier this year, HBO Max said its hockey-romance series "Heated Rivalry" averaged a whopping 10.6 million viewers per episode.

International hockey competitions, such as the league's annual "4 Nations Face-Off" midseason tournament and the 2026 Winter Olympics, have drawn huge ratings. The last "4 Nations Face-Off" game — 2025's U.S. vs. Canada final — drew 9.3 million viewers.

This year's Gold Medal men's hockey game between the U.S. and Canada averaged a stunning 26 million viewers on NBC and Peacock in the U.S., according to Nielsen data. That made it the most-watched sporting event in U.S. history with a start time before 9 a.m. ET.

"We think we've got the best content," NHL Commissioner Gary Bettman said on CNBC last month. "Our ratings have continued to increase. We were up dramatically into the double digits last year. So we view ourselves coming into the marketplace in a very strong position. Having said that, the marketplace is extremely competitive and extremely fragmented. But, I believe that with fierce competition that's going on between traditional television and the newer media platforms that there'll be a great opportunity for us to both expand our reach and get paid what we think is an appropriate reflection of our value."

Recent history suggests the NHL will get the rights increase it wants. The NBA nearly tripled its national media rights revenue, signing an 11-year, $77 billion contract with Disney, Amazon and NBCUniversal in a deal that started last season.

The NHL also renewed its TV rights deal in Canada just last year with Rogers Communications, scoring an increase of more than 100% annually when adjusting for currency fluctuations.

But recent history may not match up to present reality.

While Skydance is interested in getting a deal done, the company's top priorities are paying down debt and saving money to pay for what's going to be a huge NFL rights increase — potentially $1 billion per season or more, two of the people said.

Skydance is dead set on renewing the NFL, said one of the people. Those talks will likely occur in 2028, as the league has an opt-out clause at the end of the 2029-30 season.

Now that Paramount and WBD have been pushed together, the NHL isn't as important to Skydance for distribution leverage, which already has premier sports media rights such as the NFL, The Masters, college football, March Madness, UFC and UEFA Champions League.

That's not to say the NHL isn't a priority for Skydance. Skydance still needs programming for the cable networks it owns to keep that business chugging along. But relative to WBD, it's not as much of a must-have.

"As it relates to future rights, we'll be disciplined," Skydance CEO David Ellison said during an earnings conference call this week — perhaps hinting at his company dropping not only NHL but also other rights from the WBD portfolio in the coming months and years. "Every right has to earn its place."

Perhaps Skydance remains a partner but buys fewer NHL games or sublicenses some of them. This potentially opens the door to new bidders. CNBC's own parent company Versant would be interested in a slice of NHL games. Amazon sniffs around just about every national sports property that comes to market.

Netflix? Regular season NHL games are certainly not events. Fox? NBCUniversal? Both companies need to worry about how much it's going to cost to renew the NFL. A big package of NHL games may not fit the strategy.

How much the NHL can extract from its media partners will likely set the bar for other media rights set to come to market, including MLB, which could be delayed by labor negotiations.

Disclosure: Versant Media is the parent company of CNBC.

#2865. Ferguson 'made up' with first win as Glens boss

At the third time of asking, Duncan Ferguson is finally up and running as Glentoran manager.

After two draws to start his tenure in the Irish Premiership, a home debut for the former Everton striker had late drama under the Friday night lights at the Oval.

Jordan Stewart, dubbed the "wee man" by big Dunc after his first game, celebrated his contract extension by scoring the opening goal and, in a largely uneventful game, it looked like that would be the difference.

However, that all changed when Ben Cushnie equalised for Bangor in the 83rd minute, but there was more drama to follow as Cal Weatherup raced onto a long ball to slot home the winner with three minutes remaining and secure a first win for Ferguson as boss.

"I'm delighted to get my first three points at home, I'm absolutely made up," Ferguson said after the game.

"The boys kept on going until the very end."

Weatherup's late goal gives Ferguson first Glens win

Ferguson's first game in the Irish League was meant to be at home to Ballymena United on 24 October, but Storm Boelo and a waterlogged pitch delayed his highly-anticipated debut in the league.

Stewart scored the equaliser at then-leaders Coleraine in his first outing in the dugout the following week, and the Glens were then held to a goalless draw at Carrick Rangers.

But on Friday, Ferguson finally announced himself to the home support in east Belfast.

Despite a controlled first half and Stewart's opener, Ferguson felt that his side "sat too deep" before Bangor's equaliser, which came through former Glentoran man Cushnie.

But it was teenager Weatherup who was ultimately the difference, with a finish that Ferguson would have been proud of in his playing days as he slotted the ball between the legs of the onrushing Patrick Solis Grogan for his first goal of the campaign.

Ferguson said he was "made up" for the 18-year-old, who replaced Pat Hoban midway through the second half.

"We showed good character. We showed a bit of resilience," added the 54-year-old.

"They put us under pressure in the second half but that will happen. You're not going to dominate a game for the whole game at any level.

"I was delighted the boys showed character and resilience to get back into the game."

Given their mixed start to the season, a charge at the Irish Premiership title is going to be a long shot.

But if the Glens are to have any aspirations of a first Gibson Cup since 2009 then it is important for them to keep pace with those above.

Leaders Linfield and nearest challengers Coleraine both were comfortable winners on Friday, while Larne bounced back from their poor recent win to beat Dungannon Swifts.

Glentoran are 14 points off the Blues but have a game in hand, and they face another tricky home test against Cliftonville next Friday to try and build upon the win over the Seasiders.

As he reflected on his start to the league, Ferguson admitted "I think we need to get better overall.

"We're not conceding many goals, but we just have to create more.

"That's the hardest part of the game, but we look solid and that's the main thing.

"I'm happy with the group and the league is very close together, from top to bottom".

With that in mind, especially as he continues his early steps in the league, it's just "one game at a time" for Ferguson, rather than thinking too far ahead.

"We've just got to keep going. I think that is seven or eight games undefeated now," he added.

"We have to keep going and keep winning. We're a big club and the fans want us to keep winning, and I'm glad we got the three points for them today."

#2864. James Harden admits to being ‘James Harden’

Thirty-two points on 14 shots in a much-needed Cleveland Cavaliers win over the New York Knicks was a good reminder of who James Harden is. And in case there was still any confusion, the former MVP made sure to clear everything up afterward.

"My name is James Harden. I used to score a lot of points," Harden told reporters Wednesday evening.

That clarification was needed based on how Harden's first 11 outings had gone. He entered Wednesday's game in New York averaging an uncharacteristic 15.3 points per game, shooting a dreadful 36.4% from the field. Harden has been a step slow and unable to create space off the dribble.

There's a downstream effect to Harden not being able to score. The passing lanes dry up when defenders don't have to help cut off his drives. This has left him as an inefficient scorer and inept playmaker at the start of the season.

Wednesday, Harden showed that he still has the talent to be a dynamic scoring guard. He got the outside shot working in the second quarter, and then expanded his game from there. Defenders had to overplay him on the perimeter, which allowed him to attack inside. He used that to his advantage to score nine points in the fourth quarter, which included two finishes inside the paint.

In short, he was the Harden we've seen wreck defenses for over a decade and a half, not the one that we saw in the first 11 games this season.

Anything bought from the links helps support Fear the Sword. You can buy the comfortable Cavs hoodie HERE. You can also shop all of Homage's Cavs gear HERE.

The Cavs would be a championship contender if they could guarantee performances like this from Harden in the playoffs.

Being able to score and playmake at this level would mean teams couldn't load up to stop Donovan Mitchell, and it would create easy scoring opportunities for Jarrett Allen and Evan Mobley inside. But you can't ensure this version of Harden on a nightly basis, or even for big games he gets up for. Age catches up to every player, especially a 37-year-old like Harden who has played nearly 50,000 minutes in his 18-year career.

Instead of counting on Harden to play as he did in his prime, he's trying to find other ways to adapt his game to his current situation.

"I just focus on myself, things I need to do, and try to get in really good shape and learn my teammates and learn how to play a different brand of basketball, because I'm not the same fast person that I was like five or six years ago," Harden said.

Harden is still playing his way into shape, admitting that he wasn't as young as he used to be, so he "can't just be ready" at the start of the season. It takes a "bit of time to get adjusted to the speed, the pace, all of the above." But even when he does get back up to speed, the explosiveness isn't there for him to be the guy he was with the Houston Rockets.

Instead, Harden will need to find different ways to impact winning in this new reality he finds himself in. If someone can make that adjustment, it's a player as skilled and as smart as Harden. That adjustment won't happen overnight. There will undoubtedly be more lows like we saw in the embarrassing loss to the Charlotte Hornets earlier this week, but the Cavs are more than willing to endure those games if it means Harden can eventually find his way. Because in the end, the Cavaliers' hopes and dreams for this season and the next two depend on Harden putting the pieces together.

"Now I still score points, but I'm finding other ways to impact the game as well," Harden said. "And I think that's what people probably don't understand."

#2863. Arrowhead Pride Midseason Awards: The Chiefs’ Special Teams MVP

There are eight games in the books — with nine more to go. The Kansas City Chiefs' 2026 season reaches its midpoint in Week 10. Because the team's bye came so early in Week 5, we have decided to move our annual awards to midseason.

Nine members of the Arrowhead Pride writing staff each had one vote for our seven midseason awards, so nine votes will be tallied for each award.

Most Improved Player: DB Chris Roland-Wallace

In this post, we'll reveal the top special teams player:

VOTING: Matt Araiza (8), James Winchester (1)

On some NFL squads, special teams could feel like an afterthought. In Kansas City, a strong tradition of explosive kick and punt returns has changed the team's fortunes. The Chiefs have also boasted some of the best kickers and punters in the league over the past 30 years– along with some of the most painful missed field goals in NFL history.

Expectations are always high for the third phase in KC, as evidenced by the influence and stature of special teams coordinator Dave Toub. It's certainly been an up-and-down season (or two, or three) for Uncle Dave's group, making this a challenging operation to evaluate– or even understand. They've got a struggling kicker, a controversial kicker on the practice squad, and have a number of costly penalties seemingly every week.

We could choose a special teams player of the year in 2026 via process of elimination.

Nobody has stood out as a star on coverage ( they've been fine)

James Winchester is a solid long snapper (but it's hard to judge his impact on a game)

That really only leaves two viable options: Emmett Johnson– who has just started to come on as a kick returner OR punter Matt Araiza, who is statistically among the league leaders at his position this year.

Through eight games, Araiza has punted 25 times for 1,270 yards, averaging 50.8 yards per punt. His longest punt of the season is a 69-yard boot, and he's placed 12 punts inside the 20-yard line.

His AFC Special Teams Player of the Week performance came in the Week 4 victory over the Las Vegas Raiders. Araiza punted five times, and all five landed inside the 20-yard line– most impressive was the one downed at the Raiders' 1-yard line by Xavier Nwankpa and Emmett Johnson. But Araiza's fake punt on the first drive of the game, converting on a 4th and 4 from their own 35, is the play that we'll all remember (even if the offense did nothing with it, turning it over on downs at the 50).

The punter formerly known as "punt god" was known for highlight reel bombs in college. But, Araiza has apparently worked hard to be a complete player at his position. He's improved his touch and directional kicking, resulting in a career-best yards-per-punt average thus far.

Is Araiza the Chiefs' most valuable special teams player through the first half of the season? Let us know in the comments.

#2862. CFTC Chair Selig tells CNBC crypto industry is embracing agency rules as legislation hopes fade

The Commodity Futures Trading Commission is already seeing demand from crypto exchanges planning to opt in to its newly proposed rules governing digital assets, according to Chairman Michael Selig.

The commodities regulator in October proposed rules aimed at preventing abuse in crypto markets and codifying crypto transaction rules under a federal rulebook. In the announcement, Selig said the move was a "critical step" in ensuring the United States remains the crypto capital of the world.

Speaking this week at Cantor Convergence, an annual FinTech conference held in Miami Beach, Selig told CNBC in an exclusive interview that many of the major crypto exchanges are already registered with the CFTC because they offer financial derivatives, including perpetual futures, on their platforms and would now be able to add spot trading.

"We're also getting interest from new entrants who say, 'Look, signing up with 50 different state regimes is tough. We'd rather come to one regulator and get a license at the CFTC' so those new entrants are lining up to come register with us as well," he added.

After the Clarity Act, a comprehensive market structure bill that established a regulatory framework for digital assets, stalled in the Senate in September, the CFTC and the SEC moved to establish federal frameworks using their existing statutory authorities.

"We need to protect our markets and protect our customers using those markets so we'll continue to do exactly that in the wake of the Clarity Act failing to get the votes it needed to get to the president's desk during the current Congress," Selig said.

"Our statute does provide for authority of the agencies to register exchanges that want to offer margined, leveraged, or financed crypto assets or other types of commodity trading so we're utilizing that authority to allow our existing exchanges to begin offering these products under a regulated framework," he said.

Selig added that the oversight is opt-in for exchanges that are not currently offering margin leveraged financing, which is not permitted under existing state regimes.

But the CFTC's new rules could face new scrutiny and oversight in 2027, with Democrats set to take control of the House and Senate after the midterms. Selig told CNBC he hopes the U.S. won't see a wave of software developers moving offshore, as many crypto businesses threatened to do under the Biden administration.

"I really hope that we learned lessons from the last several years in this space," he said.

Crypto firms were at odds with Washington during the Biden administration, asking for clear operating rules, while also facing lawsuits brought by the SEC under former chair Gary Gensler's leadership. That shifted after President Donald Trump's 2024 victory.

"I do hope that we can work together with both sides of the aisle to protect software developers who want to do the right thing," Selig said, adding that those looking to commit crimes and engage in fraud should be held accountable.

When asked whether he's prepared for increased scrutiny from Congress in 2027, Selig said, "Congress will do its job investigating where it needs to investigate."

"I'm happy to answer any questions that Congress has for me just as I would answer Congress' questions today as I've done before hearing committees several times already," he said.

Selig has also faced heat from Democrats for his handling of prediction markets. In February, 23 Democratic senators sent a letter to Chairman Selig urging him to reverse the agency's greenlighting of prediction markets.

Selig has argued that prediction platforms are federally regulated as more states try to rein them in. The CFTC has sued several states to defend what the agency views as its exclusive jurisdiction to regulate event contracts, which it classifies as swaps.

"We're going to fight this up to the Supreme Court if that's where it's going," Selig said. "Our view on the statute is that we have exclusive jurisdiction over these markets, and I will not allow for states to go bring lawsuits against our exchanges, claiming that they're unlicensed gambling."

— CNBC's Tanaya Macheel contributed to this report.

Quentin Grimes is still trying to find his way in the City of Angels, and that could be even harder to do while nursing a right finger injury. The Los Angeles Lakers guard missed the last two contests, but he is seemingly ready to get back on the court.

Grimes said he expects to play in Sunday's road matchup versus the Phoenix Suns, per David Mendez-Yapkowitz of ClutchPoints. He will do his best to produce such output going forward. Considering that the finger sprain is on his shooting hand, the fan base should exercise patience. #2860. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Democrats' midterm election win has put the artificial intelligence trade in question, as voters clearly expressed opposition to the rapid buildout of data centers. The stock market could remain volatile amid AI trade concerns, macro uncertainties, and geopolitical tensions.

In this scenario, investors can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios to earn steady income. However, the extensive universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts assign ratings after in-depth financial analysis.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Viper Energy (VNOM) is a subsidiary of Diamondback and owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests, primarily in the Permian Basin. The company reported upbeat third-quarter earnings, supported by recent acquisitions and continued development activity from Diamondback and third-party operators. Viper announced a base cash dividend of 50 cents per share for Q3 2026, payable on Nov. 19. Based on the base and variable dividends announced over the past 12 months, VNOM's dividend yield stands at 5.7%.

Following the Q3 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper Energy stock with a price target of $61. The analyst noted that the company delivered solid Q3 results, with production, including oil and natural gas liquids, exceeding both TD Cowen and consensus estimates.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the key update was the announcement of two royalty acquisitions for $809 million in cash, including deferred consideration, and $82 million of newly issued shares.

Given the recently announced base dividend hike, Bilkoski expects Viper to direct incremental free cash flow toward buybacks. While the acquisitions increase leverage, the analyst believes that Viper has enough capacity for opportunistic buybacks.

Bilkoski noted that Viper has historically delivered one of the highest production per share growth profiles in his royalty coverage, but still trades at a notable discount to the royalty peer average and the company's historical FCF yield range. "In our view, its above-average growth warrants a higher valuation," said the analyst.

Bilkoski ranks No. 525 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.80%. See Viper Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Exxon Mobil (XOM) reported robust Q3 2026 profits, as its U.S. refineries boosted production to capitalize on the high diesel fuel prices sparked by the conflict in the Middle East. The oil and gas giant announced a quarterly dividend of $1.07 per share, payable on Dec. 10, reflecting a 3.9% increase. This marks Exxon's 44th consecutive year of dividend increases. At an annualized dividend of $4.28 per share, XOM offers a yield of 2.53%.

Impressed by the Q3 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Exxon stock and raised his price target to $193 from $180 to reflect higher downstream earnings, with the expectation of increased mid-cycle margins.

Gabelman noted that the strength in Exxon's refining business continues to offset Middle East-related headwinds. He added that the company increased its ownership in LNG projects, which should reduce its Middle East concentration over time from 75% to 50% and lower the likelihood of diversification-focused mergers and acquisitions. Beyond LNG, Gabelman expects Exxon's growth in the 2030s to come from Guyana, Kashagan, Permian, and possibly Venezuela.

Gabelman noted that Exxon announced a dividend hike of nearly 4% and plans to maintain buybacks at $20 billion for 2027. "The decision to maintain reduces the pro-cyclicality nature of the buyback," said the analyst.

Gabelman ranks No. 347 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.90%. See Exxon Mobil Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Williams (WMB), which owns and operates energy infrastructure to deliver natural gas, reported strong third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of $0.525 per share, or $2.10 per share annualized, payable on Dec. 28. WMB offers a dividend yield of 3.09%.

In reaction to the Q3 2026 results, CIBC analyst Robert Catellier reaffirmed a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $82. The analyst noted that Q3 results were essentially in line with expectations and reflected continued strength in execution across WMB's infrastructure portfolio.

Catellier said that Williams' Q3 performance was driven by higher transmission, storage, and power generation revenues, as well as contributions from the Momentum Midstream acquisition.

He noted that Williams maintained its full-year EBITDA guidance of $8.3 billion to $8.5 billion. In fact, results are tracking toward the higher end of the guidance range, backed by contributions from recently completed projects and the Momentum Midstream deal. WMB continues to target long-term EBITDA compound annual growth rate of at least 11% through 2030.

Catellier expects WMB's long-term targets to be supported by incremental transmission and storage opportunities, including Transco's Southeast Supply Enhancement II project and an 84 MMcf/d expansion of the Garden Connector project.

#2859. 10-year Treasury yield moves to 5.17% as traders weigh producer price report

The 10-year Treasury yield stayed over 5% on Friday after October's producer price index, a measure of wholesale prices, came in slightly less than expected.

The yield — the key benchmark for mortgages, auto loans and credit card debt — rose 4 basis points to 5.143%. It reached a high of 5.174% earlier in the day.

The 2-year Treasury note yield, typically more sensitive to short-term Federal Reserve interest rate decisions, was lower by less than 1 basis point at 4.859%. The longer-dated 30-year Treasury bond yield, sensitive to geopolitical risks, was more than 4 basis points higher at 5.472%.

One basis point equals 0.01%, and yields and prices move in opposite directions.

October's producer price index rose 0.3% versus September, less than the 0.4% expected by economists polled by Dow Jones. But on an annual basis, PPI rose 6.5% over the past 12 months, the biggest annual increase since November 2022.

There was "no silver lining" in the inflation report, said Chris Rupkey, chief economist at FWDBONDS, adding that core PPI — which excludes food, energy and trade services — increased 0.5% in October.

"It is economics 101 that producer prices lead consumer prices and right now it looks like consumers will be on the hook for a fresh burst of factory inflation," he said.

The moves come after 10-year Treasury yields jumped more than 13 basis points to more than 5% in Thursday's session, with the 2-year yield soaring more than 15 basis points.

Investors now see a Federal Reserve rate hike next month as a certainty. Traders are pricing in 100% odds of a rise at the Dec. 9 meeting, according to the CME Group's FedWatch tool. Odds of a quarter percentage point hike now stand at 89%, with the chance of a half point increase at around 14%.

Energy inflation remains a key part of the puzzle, with oil posting its second weekly decline as prospects of renewed strikes by the U.S. against Iran fade. U.S. West Texas Intermediate futures for December delivery were down 0.2% at $88.56 per barrel. Brent crude, the international oil benchmark, was higher by 0.02% at $95.84.

#2857. Packers Film Room: How Green Bay racked up interceptions

The Green Bay Packers defense generated three interceptions with sound coverage and defense in Week 9. None came with pressure on the quarterback. In fact, all three of the New England Patriots' quarterback Drake Maye's interceptions against Green Bay came from a clean pocket. The Packers never sent more than four rushers and never blitzed, yet Maye still threw three balls to a defender who was already waiting for them. What ties the picks together is what happened after the snap.

Each time, a Green Bay safety ended up somewhere Maye didn't expect, and Maye threw to the picture he had before the snap. On the first, the single-high safety didn't bite on play action.

On the second, Evan Williams was rotating over the top of what looked like one-on-one coverage. On the third, a deep-quarter safety read Maye's eyes and drove on the dig. Together, the three throws cost New England about 12.4 EPA, and two of them set up Packers touchdowns.

The first interception came on Maye's first pass of the game, on 2nd and 9 from the New England 26 with 14:32 left in the first quarter. Xavier McKinney picked it off at the 50 and returned it 36 yards to the NE 14, and Jordan Love scrambled in for a touchdown two plays later.

At minus 5.90 EPA, it was the costliest of New England's three interceptions. Green Bay was in a drop-8 Cover 3, rushing three with seven in the box. Before the snap, McKinney was the lone deep defender in the middle, about 12 yards off the ball, so the middle of the field was closed. New England ran play action from under center with a deep post and a deep crosser.

That combination is built to stress the post safety. If he carries the post, the crosser opens underneath him, and if he jumps the crosser, the post is open behind him. McKinney never took the fake. He kept his depth and stayed square in the middle third through Maye's drop, so the play action never created the conflict it was designed to.

With eight defenders in coverage, the underneath zones were full, and neither deep route had an easy window. Maye's eyes and shoulders stayed locked on the deep middle, McKinney read him the whole way, and when Maye threw the crosser 24 yards downfield, McKinney broke downhill and caught it in stride around the 50.

Play action against drop-8 Cover 3 only works if the post safety bites. McKinney didn't, so the ball went straight to the defender the concept was meant to beat. With only three rushers and a clean pocket, Maye had time to come off the deep routes and check the ball down. Charting graded it interception-worthy, with a CPOE of minus 42.

The second interception came on 1st and 10 from the New England 40 with 12:54 left in the second quarter. Maye took a deep shot down the left sideline to Kyle Williams, 37 air yards, and Brandon Cisse batted it into the air for Evan Williams to catch at the Green Bay 23. It was the only one of the three the Packers didn't score off, since they went three-and-out, and it cost minus 2.82 EPA.

Green Bay ran a WASP quads pressure with McKinney as the fourth rusher, rotating the coverage behind him. WASP usually puts a linebacker in as the 4th rusher while dropping a defensive end. On this play, McKinney is aligned more like that inside linebacker, so he's the one tasked with adding to the rush.

New England lined up in a shotgun 3×1, with a dagger concept to the trips side and Kyle Williams isolated as the X to the boundary.

Dagger uses a clear-out seam from the inside receiver and a deep dig from the outside receiver into the space the seam vacates. Before the snap, the shell looked two-high, and McKinney gave no clear sign he was coming. At the snap, he rushed, and Evan Williams rotated toward the boundary and got depth over the top of the single-receiver side.

Instead of working the dagger to the trips side, Maye took the backside shot. Cisse was in phase on the go route and Williams was closing from above, so the ball landed between them. Cisse got a hand on it at the sideline, and Williams, already overhead, caught the tip. The backside go is usually a decision made before the snap: one-on-one to the X with no safety help is a throw you take.

Here, though, the safety help only appeared after the snap as Williams rotated over. With a safety rushing, there were also fewer defenders underneath, so the dagger dig and the vacated middle were the coverage's weak spots. The Packers defense made it a contested catch because Maye put too much air under it when it should have been more on a line out to the sideline. Instead, he threw a 50/50 ball into a rotating safety.

The third interception came on 2nd and 7 from the Green Bay 44 with 5:47 left in the second quarter. Maye threw a backside dig to tight end Eli Raridon and McKinney undercut it at the Green Bay 29 and returned it for 2 yards. Christian Watson's 43-yard catch and a Kaleb Johnson touchdown run followed, making it 24-7, and the play cost minus 3.68 EPA.

Green Bay was in Cover 4 "trix," a quarters variation where the weak safety reads the inside receiver from the #3 in trips. The Packers rushed four with no blitz and six in the box, and both safeties sat 12 to 15 yards deep before the snap, with McKinney on the side the ball went to. At the snap, McKinney opened to his deep quarter, but with no vertical threat from any inside receiver, he was free to read the quarterback.

Maye finished his drop looking to the front side, then came back to the backside dig, with his head and shoulders turned before the ball came out. McKinney read the turn and drove downhill and inside, from deep-quarter depth to the spot where the dig would be caught, before Maye released it.

The throw was on time, but McKinney jumped into the throwing lane and caught it in front of Raridon at the 29. In quarters, a safety whose inside receiver doesn't go vertical can become a robber on in-breaking routes. Throwing a dig that late at that safety without a vertical route to hold him is exactly the throw the coverage is built to take away.

On these interceptions, the Packers only rushed three, four and four, and didn't get any pressure on Maye, who had time and threw every one of these picks from a clean pocket. Each pick came after the snap, when a Packers safety ended up somewhere Maye didn't expect.

McKinney stayed in the middle instead of biting on play action, Evan Williams rotated over the top of a one-on-one that wasn't, and McKinney turned a deep-quarter alignment into a robber on the dig. That's Jonathan Gannon's defense in a nutshell: show one picture, play another, and trust the safeties to beat the quarterback to the spot.

With Micah Parsons out and the injury list growing, Green Bay can't count on getting home with four. It doesn't have to when McKinney and Williams are this far ahead of the quarterback. Every passer left on the schedule will see the same disguises on film, and recognizing them on tape is a lot easier than beating them on Sunday.

#2856. Women are taking pay cuts and 'white-knuckling it' to manage menopause and work: 'I feel like I'm sitting on the sidelines'

Around 2020, Amy Terry noticed she didn't feel like her usual self.

The 50-year-old Michigander was working as a director of programming at a health association when she says she began experiencing symptoms like memory loss, low energy, mental processing difficulties — or, as she describes it, "waking up every day feeling like you were slogging through mud."

"I went from being this highly competent professional person to barely being able to get through phone calls," Terry says.

As the healthcare system strained under the weight of the Covid-19 pandemic, Terry, like many other people, struggled to get care. When she finally did, she found out it wasn't Covid-19. It was perimenopause.

Terry got through a few more years juggling her symptoms with her job, but by 2023, she couldn't hold out any longer. She quit to start independent consulting.

Women, and some trans and non-binary people, often experience career setbacks stemming from menopause that can hurt them financially and professionally. Four in 10 women who have experienced menopause or perimenopause say it has negatively affected their life or work, according to a recent survey of over 3,800 U.S. women from Gallup and Melinda French Gates' Pivotal, a group of organizations focused on social progress for women and young people.

Of them, roughly 28% felt limited in the effort they could give at work, 15% decided against pursuing a promotion or leadership role, 12% reduced their hours, and 10% took an extended leave from work or didn't return.

"This isn't just a health issue," says Jasmine Escalera, career expert at resume builder site LiveCareer. "It's a workplace issue."

A 2023 Mayo Clinic study estimated menopause symptoms drove $1.8 billion in lost work time per year in the U.S. alone.

Perimenopause, the transitional period before menopause, typically begins in one's 40s and can last several years, often bringing symptoms like hot flashes, sleep issues, irritability and lower energy, according to the Office on Women's Health. Menopause typically occurs in one's 50s and marks 12 months since a person's last period, but is often used colloquially to include the preceding perimenopause as well.

The timing of menopause can create a perfect storm. It frequently comes at a time in many women's careers when they're breaking into leadership or approaching their peak earning years, which is often between the ages of 35 and 44 for women, according to data from the Bureau of Labor Statistics. It also typically happens when many are caregiving for aging parents or raising children. This can make for an impossible choice for many women.

"Women have fought so hard for their place in the workforce," says Mimi Wied, who began perimenopause around a decade ago. "They don't want to lose it by looking like they can't handle it."

The career consequences for women "may increase the gaps we're trying to decrease," Escalera says, referring to disparities such as those in women's pay and senior-level representation relative to men. "That is a definite talent breakdown."

For Terry, becoming an independent consultant cost her employer-sponsored healthcare at the time she most needed it. It also drastically changed her income: She says she took around an 85% pay cut in the transition.

"These are supposed to be my highest-earning years," Terry says, "and I feel like I'm sitting on the sidelines."

Many women are unprepared for menopause because of social stigma around discussing it, poor health education and dismissal of women's health issues.

"I've been in the healthcare environment for 30 years; if I can't figure this out, I don't know how anyone can," says Terry.

Wied also says she didn't know what to expect. The answer quickly became apparent: The 48-year-old from Brenham, Texas, says she experienced everything from night sweats and sleeplessness to anxiety and rage.

"Sometimes I didn't feel in control of my own mouth and my body and my actions," she says.

Wied left her job as a museum docent for a secretarial role paying roughly $20,000 less when she worried her irritability would affect her capacity to help visitors, she says: "If I could have quit working, I would have."

When women do scale back at work because of menopause, they may struggle to regain that professional ground later. Terry's job switch wasn't just downshifting to a part-time role: "It's an entire change in the trajectory of your career," she says.

Factors like ageism in the job search for those of menopausal age, as well as the high cost of healthcare, particularly for common menopause treatments like hormone replacement therapy, may keep some women silently struggling through their symptoms at work.

Erin Levin is currently working through perimenopause. As a high school English teacher, the 41-year-old from San Jose, California, needs to stay on her A-game because "there is no down moment in teaching," she says.

But since starting perimenopause around two years ago, she's experienced brain fog, difficulty sleeping, and heightened anxiety and stress that make her work difficult sometimes, she says. She sometimes struggles with tasks that "were not an issue" for her a few years ago.

"Now I'm just like, God, why does it just feel so exhausting all the time?" Levin says.

Her parents help with childcare some evenings, and she tries not to schedule big tasks first thing in the morning, but it's been an adjustment. Scaling back at work isn't an option financially, she says, as a single mother sharing custody of her child.

"I still have to maintain the same appearance of having all the same skills and energy level as other people who aren't in my medical position," Levin says. "I just have to white-knuckle it."

Some women see hope in coming legislation and employer benefits offerings.

Last year, Rhode Island became the first state to enact workplace protections for women experiencing menopause. In September, Sens. Kirsten Gillibrand, D-N.Y., and Rick Scott, R-Fla., held the first congressional hearing devoted to menopause. Illinois and the city of Philadelphia have also adopted legislation taking effect in 2027 providing accommodations and protections for those in menopause.

In September, a nongovernmental organization, the International Organization for Standardization, published the first standard for developing workplace policies and practices supporting people through menstruation and menopause.

Many organizations have a ways to go in providing menopause-related benefits for their employees.

In a 2026 study from the Society for Women's Health Research and workplace menopause research organization The Fuchsia Tent, fielded among 1,100 people, around one-third of decision-makers said menopause support is currently in place or actively planned in their workplace. Fewer than 10% of employees said their organization offers menopause-specific support.

Respondents said some broader benefits, like flexible scheduling, remote options, and confidential telehealth or virtual care, would also be helpful for menopause.

Terry, Wied and Levin said they had to meet with multiple doctors before finding one who'd take their concerns seriously and who'd prescribe them hormone replacement therapy.

Levin and Wied each have a daughter. They hope better support and women's healthcare are available for their daughters when they begin menopause.

"I'm hoping that by the time my daughter goes through this, we'll have a lot more information, a lot more resources, because as it stands, it makes you super angry," Wied says.

"It's a cost on my energy, it's a cost on my stress levels, it's a cost on my bank account," Levin adds. "I don't want my daughter to have to face any of that."

As for Terry, she says she was part of a study examining potential mental health impacts of hormonal fluctuations in perimenopausal women; she hopes the research can help future generations of women. Meanwhile, her switch to independent consulting has offered her greater flexibility and a more manageable workload. But it's not perfect.

"I'm worried about retirement, I'm worried about having enough money, I'm worried about being able to continue healthcare," she says. "But I also feel like I have to take the time to feel like I'm on solid ground again."

#2855. South Anchorage and Monroe Catholic pick up gritty wins on Day 1 of 3A/4A state volleyball tournament

Nov. 13—Each of the first two matches during the opening round of the 2026 ASAA 3A/4A State Volleyball Tournament at the Alaska Airlines Center on Thursday ended in decisive three-set sweeps.

However, fans and spectators didn't have to wait much longer to be treated to a couple more entertaining games happening at the same time.

On the 3A side of the court, Monroe Catholic got the better of Sitka in four sets, taking the first handily, dropping the second and winning the third and fourth to advance to the quarterfinals.

"The second set didn't go exactly how we planned, but we just kept pushing through it, and the outcome was what we wanted, so it was really good," senior Rory Morgan said.

She was awarded the Rams' Player of the Game honors for her standout contributions, but she is especially proud of how the team came together to prevail over the Wolves.

"We've worked hard all this season, so it's nice to have won, obviously, but we still have a lot to strive for," Morgan said. "We've had a roller coaster, a lot of challenges we've had to overcome, but we've had a goal this entire year and we're going to keep shooting for that goal."

At the 4A level, South Anchorage dropped its first set to Soldotna and rallied to win each of the next three to advance.

She was awarded Player of the Game honors for the role she played in securing the Wolverines' victory.

"It feels so good," Gieser said. "This year, people thought we were going to be rebuilding, but obviously, we've shown that we are not. We're a force to be reckoned with, and that's exciting."

While it felt good to receive individual recognition, she's more excited about what the rest of the weekend holds since one loss doesn't spoil a team's chance to still win a championship in the tournament's double elimination format.

"It's great and our crowd is great," Gieser said. "I love my team and I'm just excited to be here."

Both South and Monroe Catholic went on to lose their respective quarterfinal matches in four sets later in the day but are still alive to qualify for the finals.

#2854. ‘We will sue them’: Canada threatens Stellantis as Trump trade war reshapes Jeep production plans

Canada has threatened to sue auto giant Stellantis over the planned sale of an idled assembly plant in Brampton, Ontario, alleging breaches of a federal funding agreement after the carmaker announced plans to shift production to the U.S. amid President Donald Trump's trade war.

Speaking at a news conference on Thursday, Industry Minister Mélanie Joly said the government had served Stellantis with a formal notice of default, giving the company 30 days to address its concerns or face legal action.

"We sent a notice of default and, basically, they have 30 days — or else, of course, we will sue them," Joly told reporters in Toronto.

Joly said Stellantis had violated its contractual commitments to the Canadian government and the workers at Brampton by announcing an agreement to sell the plant to armored vehicle manufacturer Roshel earlier in the week. She added that the company was now "on the hook" for hundreds of millions of dollars over the move.

Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney has described the situation at the Brampton plant as "unacceptable" and a "betrayal" of its workers.

The standoff comes as the U.S. and Canada remain locked in a bitter trade dispute, with Trump repeatedly putting pressure on automakers to move vehicle production south of the border.

Stellantis, which owns auto brands including Dodge, Fiat, Chrysler and Peugeot, had scrapped plans to build its Jeep Compass at the Brampton plant and shifted future production to a factory in Illinois amid U.S. auto tariffs and other market pressures.

Stellantis Canada CEO Trevor Longley said in a statement shared with CNBC that the company would return $24 million it received in government support for the Brampton Assembly's original retooling plan. This amount, however, falls significantly short of the Canadian government's own estimate of what the company owes.

"We recognize the disappointment and uncertainty surrounding the future of the facility. While our decision to sell the plant represents a change from the original plans for Brampton, the agreement we have advanced with a Canadian manufacturer provides a path to return the facility to productive industrial use," Longley said.

"As Canada's largest automotive employer, this decision also allows Stellantis to focus its resources on sustaining competitive manufacturing operations in Canada in the face of challenging market realities," he added.

Stellantis said Monday that it had agreed to sell the Brampton assembly plant to Roshel for an undisclosed amount.

It also acknowledged its obligations to employees at the facility and said it remains prepared to work with Unifor, Canada's largest private sector union, to negotiate a settlement to assist union-represented employees.

Stellantis stock has fallen sharply since the start of the year, with its Milan-listed shares down more than 60% for 2026 so far.

— CNBC's Michael Wayland contributed to this report.

#2853. Preview: Jazz looking to take liftoff in NBA Cup Game

Here we are again. We here at SLC Dunk can whisper to you sweet dreams that each game is an encouraging sign that the team is trending in the right direction, but the harsh reality is the Jazz are 1-11. Games are more about keeping your dignity and pride as an organisation, steering clear of the team's worst start in franchise history (1-17), when you're trying to win games. The musty odor the Jazz emit with each fourth-quarter meltdown escalates.

Hopes of a late-blooming win streak were swatted yet again on Wednesday, when the middling Orlando Magic forced a 21-5 run at the Delta Center without a single Jazz timeout. Will Hardy had the young fella's play through their mistakes, which blew up right in their faces. This was a common culprit in how the Jazz lost games during their tanking years, but it simply cannot carry over in a supposed 'winning' season.

The lucky opponent that gets to face the Jazz is the 9-3 Houston Rockets; both teams will play their second game of the inaugural NBA Cup, where Utah has already fallen 0-1 in their first match against the Dallas Mavericks. The Rockets, on the other hand, are also 0-1. To claim one of the NBA Cup's 'wildcard' spots, both teams will essentially need to be undefeated the rest of their games while also tallying up a high point differential to beat out the others.

Houston has followed the NBA's trend of moving past dying minimalism, rebranding with a new uniform set featuring ketchup and mustard to honor the Hakeem Olajuwon era. Their on-court play has transformed them into a formidable Western Conference contender by focusing on a two-way balance. They post a top-five offensive rating (119.3) and allow 106.5 points a game (4th in the NBA). The Jazz will need to play better than perfect to avoid being locked in the doghouse. It can't be too hard, right? After all, they won't have to be hand-checked by former DPOY Marcus Smart; he's suspended for throwing a mean right hook at the face of Charles Bassey.

Wednesday night saw Franz Wagner be the man of the hour by scoring 12 of his career-high 38 points in the fourth quarter, blowing a kiss to the Utah crowd in his finishing act. The Jazz were vulnerable defending the pick-and-roll, leaving driving lanes open that allowed Wagner to viciously attack the paint.

The Rockets' brand of basketball generares a massive share on physical size and high-volume two-point scoring. Houston averages 12.9 offensive rebounds per game, led by 6'7" Amen Thompson grabbing 3.1 offensive boards a game.

All-Star Alperen Şengün dictates the team's gravity out of the post and elbows, pacing the squad with 21.1 points, 9.4 rebounds and 7.9 assists. He experienced some inefficiency early on in the season, but his footwork and passing vision are something to be cautious about; Utah can't be caught slipping on backdoor cuts.

The clearest path to exploiting Houston is to attack their secondary. Early-season injuries have left Steven Adams and Clint Capela sidelined, forcing them to play deep on their bench, where we'll likely see our old pal Oscar Tshiebwe weather the storm. Utah's second unit is as unpredictable as ever. They can blow a double-digit lead in minutes, or they can also hold down the fort and score 41 bench points against the Orlando Magic. The key is to wear down the non-Şengun minutes by punishing smaller lineups, putting Utah in a strong position to stealan NBA Cup Group Stage matchup.

Truly, the last week has seen some ridiculous stuff from 19-year-old Darryn Peterson.

The Jazz rookie broke out in his latest scoring surge, averaging 23.5 points, 4.0 rebounds and 4.5 assists on 47/38/92 shooting splits over his last three games. I mean, a near 50-40-90 from a 19-year-old rookie is pretty good, right?

Peterson also set a new NBA career high with 26 points against the Timberwolves on November 9, scoring in all four quarters without attempting a single free throw. Alongside his efficient shooting (39.4% from three over the four games), he chipped in on defense with five steals and four blocks total, highlighted by a three-block effort in Dallas.

Utah leaned more on Peterson as its No. 1 option this week after Keyonte's injury, unlocking higher scoring volume while maintaining his playmaking ability. His season scoring average jumped to 16.3 points per game, moving him to No. 4 on the latest NBA.com Kia Rookie Ladder, up from 6th.

Win or lose, Darryn Peterson playing a good brand of basketball is what brings in tickets. If all else fails, the one thing the Utah Jazz do have is their faith in their #2 pick. As long as he's on the court, there's at least one Jazz fan smiling at their TV, because the future has never been this bright for a kid from Canton.

All we've heard about Peterson. Before that, Keyonte George had to carry scoring duties on his back in the midst of Utah's injuries to their frontcourt. It was only recently that Ace Bailey finally began to regain his stride. But the issue is that Utah needs one more guy to step up — someone who makes just enough of a contribution so it doesn't feel like Utah has to fight tooth and nail with .500 teams every single night.

Is it Isaiah Collier? Was it really all it took- a banged-up shoulder for him to have his Thunderstruck moment? I should remind you he hadn't hit a single triple all season before his perfect 4-for-4 night over Miami. That was then followed by another made three-ball against the Magic. In all honesty, all that's needed is for Collier to shoot an amount that at least has his opponent respect his three-point range. The dream is for him to learn how to play more off-ball, while simultaneously addressing his weaknesses in navigating ball screens and managing overall defensive rating metrics

Brice Sensabaugh's role has continually fluctuated through this twelve-game opening stretch. His 23 minutes against the Magic were the most he's played all season. Sure, Brice can shoot the rock. But the harsh truth is he's been unproductive in every other facet of the game. He can't swing the ball; his defense negatively impacts the team, and has shown some really discouraging off-ball lapses. If his shot isn't falling, then that really hurts the Jazz. Having Brice be the 'next man up' means he should be hunting quick-trigger catch-and-shoot threes that could hopefully have a butterfly effect in swinging the game's momentum.

Svi Mykhailiuk has been through an otherworldly shooting funk. I consider myself a Svi truther more than others, but he's had a longer leash compared to other years, despite his 28/21/50 scoring splits—no, that's not a typo. Averaging an honest 16.1 minutes per game, really, shooting above 50% from the field should be the realistic goal.

When is Will Hardy finally going to acknowledge his two-way guys? The expectation was that Kobe Bufkin and Harrison Ingram were going to get their shine after Lauri Markkanen was sidelined, but that moment has yet to come. Instead, Will Hardy opts to reduce his already short-handed rotation, staggering Darryn Peterson and Ace Bailey with the second unit. But when you're 1-11, you really just have to try anything in your arsenal. You don't have time to see if the guys on a cold streak can 'work through their struggles'. Now, it's about who can contribute right this second.

#2852. CNBC's Official NHL Team Valuations 2026: How the 32 franchises stack up

For the first time, hockey team valuations have crossed the $4 billion threshold, with CNBC's NHL valuations led by the Toronto Maple Leafs, at $5.4 billion, the New York Rangers, at $4.7 billion, and the Montreal Canadiens, at $4.1 billion. The average NHL team is now worth $2.71 billion, 23% more than a year ago.

Don't be fooled by the lone NHL control sale to close over the past 12 months: the $1.8 billion sale of the Pittsburgh Penguins to the Hoffmann family. Although the transaction was approved by the league in June, the price was negotiated about 18 months ago.

Look instead at the latest minority stake transactions. In March the NHL approved the sale of a 12.5% stake in the Carolina Hurricanes by Tom Dundon in a transaction that valued the team at $2.66 billion. Scott Malkin and other shareholders are close to selling a 15% stake in the New York Islanders at a $3 billion valuation. Sports bankers tell CNBC these deals are representative of what control stakes in the two teams would sell for given the hot market for sports teams.

"Like the other major sports leagues there is a strong demand to own hockey teams because their media content values are high and there is a scarcity of teams," said Sal Galatioto, whose firm, Galatioto Sports Partners, advises clients on both the buy and sell sides of sports team transactions. "Capitalism and technology are creating more wealth and the NHL, like the NFL, NBA and MLB, is an industry with a very long product life cycle, a hedge against risk in AI."

The NHL's current national media rights deals with Walt Disney and Warner Bros. Discovery are worth a combined annual average of close to $650 million through the 2027-28 season, but given the league's strong television ratings last season, a big increase could be in the offing.

"The 2025-26 NHL regular season averaged 546,000 viewers across ESPN/ABC and TNT Sports, up 25% from last year and the highest average in more than a decade," Sports Media Watch wrote. "The 25% increase is on the high end of a general rise in sports viewership that includes gains of 35% for the NBA, 19% for men's college basketball and 10% for the NFL."

Sports Media Watch also wrote that the 2026 NHL Stanley Cup Playoffs averaged 1.8 million viewers in the U.S. across ESPN/ABC and TNT Sports. That's up 68% from 2025, and it's the highest average on record for the playoffs on American television, going back to 1994, it said.

The NHL is pondering adding teams in cities such as Houston and Atlanta, and another factor fueling NHL valuations is the belief that the expansion fees paid by the new teams would surpass $2 billion. The Seattle Kraken, the NHL's most recent expansion team, paid a $650 million fee to join the league for the 2021-22 season.

According to CNBC's figures, revenue for the league's 32 teams averaged $257 million for the 2025-26 season, 6% more than the previous year, while EBITDA — earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization — averaged $48 million per team, a decline of 11% due to arena expenses. The league's profitability should rebound as money spent on hospitality, concessions and merchandise stores boosts revenue.

In April 2025 the NHL and Rogers Communications agreed to a 12-year national Canadian media rights deal worth $7.79 billion, based on exchange rates as of Nov. 24, that will begin with the 2026-27 season. That's more than twice the amount the league is getting from its existing 12-year deal with Rogers. This will help push up the league's hockey-related revenue — which includes media rights, sponsorships, premium seating, tickets, concessions and parking — to around $7.25 billion for the 2026-27 season, 7% more than last season.

CNBC's Official NHL Team Valuations are current enterprise values — equity plus net debt — calculated using revenue multiples, and include the economics of the team's arena, including non-NHL revenue that accrues to the team's owner. The valuations are meant to represent what a control stake valuation would go for in an arm's-length transaction and do not include the value of the arena or the real estate it sits on.

Revenue figures are net of revenue sharing and the 35% of home playoff non-premium gate revenue that goes toward the league's revenue-sharing pool. Revenue multiples are based on historical transaction prices, as well as what investors, league executives and bankers say about the current market.

CNBC values exclude other businesses related to the teams with separate financial statements, such as the Edmonton Oilers' mixed-use real estate project. For teams that sell their own merchandise, such as the Oilers, CNBC deducts the cost of goods sold from merchandise sales. Likewise, CNBC excludes the market value of affiliates/equity method investments to ensure consistent enterprise value-to-revenue and enterprise value-to-EBITDA multiples. Debt figures are the latest available and include both team debt and stadium debt.

CNBC incorporates the value of a pending new arena in our valuations if construction has begun and financing has been secured. For example, the $2.25 billion valuation for the Calgary Flames incorporates their new arena, which is scheduled to open in time for the 2027-28 season. Likewise, CNBC valuations anticipate a substantial increase in the NHL's next U.S. national broadcasting deal, which would begin with the 2028-29 season.

Revenue and EBITDA figures are for the 2025-26 season and net of revenue sharing and player escrow. CNBC presents revenue and operating income figures on a cash basis, rather than an accrual basis. CNBC figures are different from the league's official hockey-related revenue because we include non-NHL arena revenue that goes to the teams. When the owner of the arena also owns both the NHL team and NBA team that play in the same building — as is the case for several teams, including Toronto's Maple Leafs and Raptors, who are both owned by Rogers Communications and play in Scotiabank Arena — we divide revenue from non-sports events held at the arena evenly between the two teams.

All valuation figures are in U.S. dollars. The exchange rate for revenue and EBITDA is based on a one-year average, from June 2025 to June 2026, of CA$1 = US$0.72, and the exchange rate for team values and debt, as of Sept. 21, is CA$1 = US$0.71.

Sources for CNBC's Official NHL Team Valuations include team owners, investors and executives, as well as sports bankers and league consultants; public documents such as stadium lease agreements, stadium authority budget audits and credit rating reports; and sponsorship and broadcasting industry executives. Certain figures were provided by league executives who spoke to CNBC on the condition of anonymity because agreements are private and they do not have permission to speak about them publicly. Figures that could not be confirmed with sources are CNBC estimates.

— CNBC's Jungwoo Ethan Kim contributed to this report.

#2851. U.S. seizes $1.2 billion in cryptocurrency stolen from Binance

U.S. authorities have seized around $1.2 billion in virtual assets stolen from cryptocurrency exchange Binance last month, the Department of Justice said Thursday, as it vowed action to tackle cyberscams originating from North Korea.

Over $6 billion was stolen from industry giant Binance on Oct. 29, which the DOJ attributed to actors linked to the North Korean military.

Collective efforts between the U.S. government and private sector have resulted in more than $2.7 billion of that amount being frozen.

The FBI blames the attack, which shook confidence in crypto market security, on members of the North Korean military it refers to as "TraderTraitor."

The DOJ said it was taking coordinated action against what it called the North Korean government's "schemes to fund its regime through the massive theft of cryptocurrency."

That includes "the court-authorized seizure and restraint of more than $1.2 billion worth of cryptocurrency recently stolen from Binance," it said.

DOJ units, law enforcement partners and virtual asset service providers have been working to intercept the stolen assets.

But efforts to track and freeze the assets are ongoing, as TraderTraitor actors continue to launder them through various cryptocurrency channels.

Authorities have also seized cloud-based infrastructure used in a massive identity impersonation scam known as "FaceStealer," which has seen more than $400 million worth of cryptocurrency stolen over 18 months, the DOJ said.

The scheme sees hackers set up fake video meetings using stolen accounts and recordings of people known to the target by using AI-generated deepfakes. The hackers then say a technical problem requires a fix, install malware on a computer and take over the victim's communications, steal their cryptocurrency and target their contacts — sometimes months or more than a year after the initial compromise.

Individuals working in the cryptocurrency and web3 industries are prime targets.

Assistant Director in Charge Patrick Grandy, of the FBI's Los Angeles Field Office, called FaceStealer "a highly effective social engineering scheme capable of deceiving even the most experienced technical and security conscious computer user."

"It exploits our trust in ubiquitous video conference platforms and valued personal relationships. If you can't imagine a scenario where you might fall prey to it, you are not thinking creatively enough," Grandy added.

"North Korea's government bankrolls its rogue regime and illicit weapons program through cryptocurrency theft," Principal Deputy Assistant Attorney General for National Security M. Scott Proctor said in a statement.

"America will fight back against that corrupt regime's effort to profit through cybercrime. The National Security Division will use every tool at its disposal to seize and return North Korea's ill-gotten gains."

#2849. Jensen Huang scandal casts doubt on Nvidia’s AI dominance — how we got here

Nvidia is facing one the biggest crises in its 33 year history, with founder Jensen Huang resigning as CEO Thursday after multiple reported affairs, including one with a woman alleged to be a Chinese spy. Now CFO Colette Kress has taken the helm as Nvidia's interim CEO.

The implications of Huang's affair with Alice Huang run far deeper than infidelity. That's because her ties to Nvidia customer Megaspeed put her in the middle of a sprawling effort to accumulate AI chips across Southeast Asia. And Megaspeed is under investigation for possibly diverting restricted Nvidia chips into the hands of Chinese companies.

The relationship between Jensen Huang and Alice Huang dates back to 2023, according to The New York Times. That was a "turbulent" period for Nvidia, Jensen Huang told CNBC during an interview at the time. Nvidia was in the midst of redesigning some chips to comply with the first in a series of strict export controls imposed by the U.S. government that restricted sales to China.

"The regulation is going to be just fine. We're going to be able to work through it," Huang told CNBC from Nvidia's headquarters in February 2023. The interview was for a documentary about how Huang created Nvidia and built it into the backbone of the AI boom.

That same day, CNBC also interviewed Kimberly Powell, Nvidia's vice president of healthcare, with whom Jensen Huang reportedly had an affair.

It's not the first time Nvidia has faced an uncertain future. The company nearly went bankrupt in the early years after it was founded in 1993.

"Every company makes mistakes and I make a lot of them," Huang said in the 2023 interview. "And some of them put the company in peril."

In the early months of the AI boom, after OpenAI launched ChatGPT in November 2022, Nvidia was on the precipice of historic growth. Jensen Huang told CNBC he'd like to keep running the company for "three or four decades," as long as "I feel that I'm making a real contribution to the company."

#2848. Trump says he wants to ‘get Cuba done’ before leaving office, would consider military force

U.S. President Donald Trump has said he wants to "get Cuba done" before leaving office in 2029, adding that he would "certainly consider" military intervention against the communist-run Caribbean island.

In an interview with conservative radio host Hugh Hewitt on Thursday, Trump said his administration was monitoring the situation in Havana "very closely" and described Cuba as a "failed" state.

"I want to get Cuba done before I leave," Trump said. His comments marked the first time he has put a timeline on possible military intervention to topple Cuban authorities, according to Politico.

Trump said Secretary of State Marco Rubio, whose parents are from Cuba, "would love to do it" and said he would "certainly consider" a short military operation.

It comes as the U.S. continues to enforce a comprehensive economic embargo against Cuba, a campaign which has contributed to a deepening energy and economic crisis on the island. Cuba has suffered repeated nationwide blackouts in recent months, as well as fuel and food shortages.

The Cuban Embassy in Washington, D.C., estimated on Thursday that the cost of the U.S. blockade to Cuba between March 2025 and February 2026 amounted to $8 billion.

Earlier this month, the Treasury Department imposed further sanctions on Cuban oil tankers and shipping companies and threatened to target any international entity or person doing business within its marine or maritime industries as part of a push "to end the Cuban regime's malign activities, both in Cuba and across our hemisphere."

In response, Cuba's Embassy in Washington, D.C., said these steps "deepen an energy siege that already leaves hospitals without reliable power and families facing blackouts. The blockade is not targeted at a government. It is collective punishment that hits ordinary people every day."

Trump has previously talked up the prospect of a "friendly takeover" of Havana and said the White House could turn its sights on Cuba after the Iran war. The U.S. president has also said he could do anything he wanted with the country, adding that he thinks he will have the "honor" of "taking Cuba."

In May, Cuban Energy Minister Vicente de la O Levy said the country of roughly 10 million had run out of fuel oil and diesel, describing the country's predicament as "extremely tense."

Analysts have typically been skeptical about the prospect of a U.S. military campaign against Cuba, suggesting that further economic pressure against the island's authorities appears more likely.
