#2847. How Alaska's prior 3A volleyball finalists took different paths back to state this year

Nov. 13—Less than 20 miles separate the communities of Kenai and Nikiski in Southcentral Alaska.

Their high school varsity volleyball teams have combined to claim the last four 3A state titles and have faced off in the finals in each of the last two years, with the Kenai Central Kardinals topping the Nikiski Bulldogs to win the 2025 title.

Both perennial contenders are back at this year's state tournament, which runs through Saturday at the Alaska Airlines Center in Anchorage. But the paths they took to get back to the sport's biggest stage were polar opposites.

Last year, Kenai was powered to the program's third championship in the last four seasons by All-State senior outside hitter Sophie Tapley, who is now a freshman at the University of Alaska Anchorage.

Instead of looking for one player to step up and fill the star-shaped void Tapley left in their lineup, the Kardinals have collectively elevated their game to make up for the loss.

"It's been a whole team sort of effort," head coach Tracie Beck said. "I have a libero that's a senior and a setter that's a senior, and so those guys are kind of the base, but all of them have stepped up in different ways, especially those young ones."

In their opening-round sweep of Barrow in this year's tournament Thursday morning, Beck specifically praised the efforts of freshman Caitlyn Cramer for stepping up as a server, a trait many of her underclassmen share.

"They're serving consistently and getting points by themselves at the serving line," Beck said. "It's crucial. It's kind of taking the whole team where we had a big gun that we could look to and shut things down the last few years. So the team is definitely well-rounded. Everybody has to play their role for us to find success."

As for top-seeded Nikiski, a major catalyst for the team's success has been the prolific contributions of 6-foot junior middle blocker Blakeley Jorgensen.

"Blakeley is a great player and that's definitely who they rely on when times are tough," Beck said. "In small towns, we don't get big players like 6-footers, 5-9, 5-10, 5-11."

Jorgensen's 529 kills entering the state tournament lead the Bulldogs by nearly 200, and her 109 total blocks reflect dozens more than her next closest teammate.

"I think just having that next-play mentality and letting go of mistakes is one thing that helps me," Jorgensen said.

She was a freshman on the 2024 Bulldogs team that won state and added to her state tournament Player of the Game certificate collection on Thursday by leading the Nikiski to a three-set sweep of Nome-Beltz, marking the fourth of her career.

"It's really nice getting these, but I just play for my teammates, and I just want to support them," Jorgensen said. "Especially my seniors. We have eight this year, so it's a lot."

[South Anchorage and Monroe Catholic pick up gritty wins on Day 1 of 3A/4A state volleyball tournament]

Behind her for the second-most kills and blocks on the team is senior Mandee Roofe, who was injured in last year's state tournament and is another powerful presence in the middle of the net at 5-foot-11.

Her mindset heading into the rest of the tournament is to "play hard and have fun," with less emphasis on trying to avenge last year's unsuccessful title defense.

"We're just going to try our hardest and go out strong," Jorgensen said. "Right now we've got some injured players, some sick players too, so we're trying to play through that and play as a team and work together."

Kenai and Nikiski faced off four times heading into the state tournament, with the Kardinals holding the edge in the head-to-head record at 3-1.

"I love playing Nikiski," Beck said. "They're our neighbors and (Nikiski head coach) Stacey (Segura) and I are very competitive. I respect what she has done with that program. She was here longer than me, so I tease her about being the old goat around."

The two teams and coaching staffs know each other so well that their matches have the feel of a friendly intersquad scrimmage rather than a fierce rivalry grudge match.

"It's a fun matchup and it's always great to play them," Beck said. "You know them about as well as you do your own players."

#2846. The Mavericks are reaping the benefits of what Dusty May calls the trust factor

The winds of change. The transformation of the Dallas Mavericks from last year to this is astounding. Everyone watching can feel it – something major has changed. It's not just the leadership, a lot of staff and some of the roster. There's more to it.

It is, however, all those new people, and what they brought along with them, that started the winds of change.

The palpable energy shift. The intangible vibe change and the excitement around the team, on the team, for the team. Even through the screen. In the AAC, I bet it's even more evident.

Everything has changed in Dallas. How the basketball looks on the court, how the players move (most of the time when they don't revert back to bad habits), how they communicate, how the bench acts, even how the admin works and how the fans respond to it. But most importantly, how the coaches act, react and interact.

Dusty May has, along with his staff and new GM's Masai Ujiri and Mike Schmitz, brought a culture change to Dallas that everyone is talking about.

Nov 9, 2026; Dallas, Texas, USA; Dallas Mavericks head coach Dusty May talks with guard Kyrie Irving (11) during the second half against the Indiana Pacers at the American Airlines Center. Mandatory Credit: Jerome Miron-Imagn Images | IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

From what I see, it's a culture change founded on one of the most important aspects of leadership and sports: trust.

The power of trust and all it entails and doesn't entail. Building trust on a sports team means a higher rate of passing, which creates a more dynamic offense and easier scoring, which we see in the increased number of assists this season, among other things. It means trusting that the ball will come back to you if you move well off-ball, and it means trusting your teammates to score, as well. That is healthy for a team as a whole, and wouldn't ya know – it helps create a winning mindset and a winning team.

Trust also means that you don't fear making mistakes as much, because you trust your coach and teammates won't punish you for it, and they trust you will make up for it (perhaps on defense). Being fearful of making mistakes is the number one killer of creativity and joy. And what happens when you don't have that? Last season happens.

And trust means that you know that your coach will make the right decisions for the team and yourself, you trust that they're not out to get you and you trust that you are liked on our team. On Dusty May's team, the players know he has their best interests at heart.

Dusty May is a relationship-led or based coach. He coaches his players so well, because he knows them and they trust him. I wrote more about my early impression of his coaching recently in this piece.

Dusty May has talked about trust a lot. Recently, he showed us how to talk a team up, and how he puts an emphasis on trust:

"What a problem, where you're in a pickle, where two guys like that (Cooper Flagg and Kyrie Irving) just got it going and you just gotta rotate between putting the ball in each other's hands and knowing that they're going to make the plays. So obviously, what a luxury for us."

"And once again, they both trust their teammates. When Houston put two on the ball and they were determined to take it out of their hands. They continue to trust and trust and trust, and then those reps and that experience, those guys handled it better… and that's a sign of the trust factor that Coop and Kyrie have with their teammates."

It's not a stretch to say that the Mavs have had more exciting and promising wins already than the entirety of last season, while the vibes have been all the way up to 2024 Finals run levels at times.

Oh, the 2024 playoffs energy, do you remember? The power of trust and the power of a winning culture is back in Dallas. Back then, a lot of it was based on Luka Doncic and Kyrie Irving leading and establishing a culture, where everyone trusted them and each other. Now, Kyrie Irving being back has helped tremendously – and Dusty May, his coaching staff and the new leadership has done the rest, while the players came in with all the right intentions and energy.

To put in perspective how well the Dallas Mavericks actually are looking when it comes to creating this culture needed for winning, let's take a look at what is happening on another NBA team right now. NBA teams are all full of good players. The best in the world. So why do some collapse on themselves or crash and burn, despite often having stars, superstars and so-called good coaching?

In Cleveland, we have an example of what happens when trust is lacking. Here, things are looking quite differently compared to Dallas. And it's not just about who has the best players. The opposite, actually.

In this first example from a couple weeks ago, Mario Hezonja, an actual superstar from the EuroLeague, who's also a leader and very high-IQ player, was open for a corner three, which would have been the best look in this possession. A great look actually, considering the fact that he's an elite threepoint shooter.

Donovan Mitchell, however, the guy who's supposed to be the leader of this team, looks like he decides that it's his shot now, and cuts it off to take – or steal – the shot. Which he misses.

Do you see any trust here? I don't think so. But this is worse than that. It's obstruction. It's about your own ego over the team.

This is not just a show of bad vision and basketball IQ, it's also poor leadership and probably a way to try and dominate Hezonja in the hierarchy.

Because what happened just a few days earlier exposed Mitchell in a way he is clearly not used to. To explain the background a little, Hezonja is accustomed to being the leader and superstar, it is something he has earned through the years. On top of that, he understands basketball at a high level, which becomes clear after this game. So back to the play: he waves Mitchell away to punish a mismatch a few days before. Whether this "steal" was retaliation for that, we will never know. But it was not a good look for anyone.

Hezonja showed his intelligence and basketball knowledge with this and explained matter-of-factly afterward that Mitchell should not bring the ball up, a humble stance to take from a European superstar – who is also more direct (as we are wont to be).

"I told him you're not supposed to bring the ball up under pressure. I waived JT out to go 1-on-1 against my mismatch. I spoke to James and Donovan, there's five of us on the court and you need to read those mismatch situations."

This completely exposed the lack of awareness on this team, however. And it may explain some animosity. But by media and especially fans in the comments, though, it was widely heralded as something that needed to be done to address the issues on that team. A winning mindset on a team with no trust can be disruptive. But sometimes that's what's needed.

Back to Dallas, where we see a stark contrast in play style. Good energy and vibes bring trust (and the other way around), and that translates to better looks and eventually – winning basketball.

"There's a lot of trust there knowing, if I give it up to you, you're going to give it back," Devin Harris of the Mavs broadcast crew said in this clip, hitting the nail on the head.

And it was this possession in particular that had me excited about what the Mavs can do this season. Ya, there are superstars and they can all iso and score at turn. But can they play together? Can they trust each other to make the right read?

When a team trusts each other, special things start happening. That's where the magic shows up. We know what it looks like, and we know what it feels like, because we saw it in the 2024 Finals run. The Dallas Mavericks had something special brewing then, something that made them better as a whole than their individual parts.

That feeling seems to slowly return to Dallas these days. Sometimes we need a reminder of what not to do, before we realize what to do instead. And maybe that's what happened in Dallas last season. The struggles of Mario Hezonja and the Cleveland Cavaliers as a contrast is a great reminder of how well things are going.

The winds of change. Trust in Dallas is back.

#2845. Apple, Alphabet and Uber survived major founder transitions. Nvidia will be a harder test

Jensen Huang is not the first CEO to leave a company he helped build.

But experts told CNBC that his sudden resignation from Nvidia at a critical juncture is unique and leaves his eventual successor with challenges in leading the company that's powering the artificial intelligence boom.

In 33 years, Huang, who resigned on Thursday amid reports of extramarital affairs, including with a woman U.S. intelligence believes is a spy for China, made the company he co-founded the world's most valuable.

Former Goldman Sachs CEO Lloyd Blankfein wrote on X that he was "indispensable" but "the graveyards are filled with indispensable men," referencing a quote attributed to French leader Charles de Gaulle.

While the board looks for a new CEO and CFO Colette Kress does the job temporarily; analysts say Huang's charisma and bullish drive will be hard to replicate.

"I'm not sure we've seen a situation quite like this, where the founder and CEO of one of the world's largest companies faces such a sudden departure amid scandal," Michael Withers, professor of management at the University of Notre Dame's Mendoza College of Business, told CNBC.

Whoever follows will "face challenges that go well beyond those of a typical CEO replacing a founder," he added.

Apple's image was inseparable from Steve Jobs when he resigned in 2011. But unlike Nvidia, the company had had other CEOs — and an internal successor.

Tim Cook invested in Apple's chip strategy and services business and became a key corporate diplomat in dealings with the U.S. and Chinese governments. He was known as an operator who controlled Apple's supply chain.

Cook had "considerable preparation" for becoming CEO, Withers said. Apple's market cap went from about $350 billion to $4 trillion in his 15-year tenure.

Larry Page and Sergey Brin left their executive roles at Alphabet in 2019, 21 years after founding Google.

Page and Brin's departures were expected. Page said at the time that it was the "natural time" to simplify the company's management structure. Alphabet shares are up more than 300% since then under Sunder Pichai.

Travis Kalanick left Uber in 2017 after mounting pressure over a series of scandals and governance concerns.

But his departure "unfolded over several months, and while it certainly attracted attention, it pales in comparison to what Nvidia is currently facing," Withers added.

Former Expedia CEO Dara Khosrowshahi repaired the organization and found a path to profitability.

Withers said successor CEOs often struggle "under normal circumstances" to juggle maintaining what made a company successful while establishing their own leadership.

"It becomes considerably more difficult when the transition follows such a high-profile scandal involving the company's founder," he added.

Huang had an extraordinary 60 direct reports, as part of a flat structure that favored fast communication over layered hierarchies.

Withers told CNBC Nvidia did not appear to have a succession plan for Huang, who is also often described as inextricably linked to the company.

"We're talking about a company that was, until recently, the largest in the world by market capitalization. That's a pretty remarkable situation," he added.

As well as having to "step out of his shadow," the new CEO will have to deal with the "scandal" arising from Huang's conduct and "the extent to which it sticks with Nvidia," Withers said.

Huang's successor will also have to tackle the longer-term business strategy, while picking up the relationships he managed.

Huang cultivated a close relationship with U.S. President Donald Trump and accompanied him to China in May. Huang consistently pushed for the U.S. to relax export restrictions that prevent Nvidia from selling its chips to the world's second-largest economy.

He also managed close relationships with the bosses of the company's key suppliers, from SK Hynix to Samsung.

Storm Uru, co-head of the Global Innovation team at Nvidia investor Liontrust Asset Management, said two key priorities were changing company culture and managing the supply chain.

The supply chain was "where Jensen was able to provide a vision for the future and able to bring people to the table and to expand capacity to enable the build-out of this technology," Uru told CNBC, adding it mattered especially "when supply chains are already so tight."

Nvidia continues to face rising competition from rivals such as AMD and hyperscalers, which are ramping up efforts to develop their own chips.

Investors noted that the new CEO will have a roadmap of products for the next few years, unaffected by Huang's removal.

"We have to look beyond Jensen ... there are thousands upon thousands of engineers," Joanne Feeney, partner and portfolio manager, Advisors Capital Management, told CNBC on Thursday, as pressure was mounting on Huang to go.

#2844. Sam Hartman NFL contract: What the new 'Bachelor' earns with Commanders

For the second time ever, "The Bachelor" is going to be an active NFL player.

Washington Commanders quarterback Sam Hartman has been named the new star for Season 30 of ABC's reality dating franchise. The 27-year-old admitted during an appearance on "Good Morning America" it was the previous NFL player to serve as "Bachelor" who helped convince him to hand out roses to 30 women seeking a romantic relationship on national television.

Jesse Palmer, who was named the host of "The Bachelor" in 2021, was a member of the New York Giants when he starred on the show. Palmer ended the season in a relationship with winner Jessica Bowlin, but they split a month after the May 2004 finale.

"He kind of gave me the insight, what it is, what it's not," Hartman said of Palmer. "I'm excited but also very nervous."

Palmer appeared in eight NFL games during his career, though none came after his stint as "The Bachelor." Hartman has mainly been on Washington's practice squad during his three NFL seasons.

Here's a closer look at Hartman's Commanders contract, how NFL practice squads work and his NFL career to date.

NFL HOT SEAT RANKINGS: Coaches most likely to be fired

Hartman was part of the Commander's final preseason roster cuts in August 2026 and then re-signed to the team's practice squad for the third-straight year. He is to set to make $247,500 in base salary if he remains with Washington's practice squad for the entire 2026 NFL season based on the league's collective bargaining agreement.

Experience determines how much money a practice squad player can make.

Those with more than two seasons of experience can negotiate contracts with a salary range between $18,350 and $22,850 per week, according to the NFL's collective bargaining agreement. That equates to a salary between $330,300 and $411,300 if a player were to remain on a team's practice squad for the entire season. Players with fewer than two seasons will make $13,750 per week in 2026, or $247,500 for the entire season.

Hartman falls into the latter category because he has yet to appear in an NFL game. Contracts for players on the practice squad do not count towards an NFL team's salary cap.

"The Bachelor" Season 30 with Sam Hartman will premiere March 21, 2027, at 8 p.m. ET/PT on ABC.

Hartman began his professional career in 2024 after playing his final collegiate season at Notre Dame in 2023. He also played five seasons at Wake Forest and went undrafted in the 2024 NFL Draft. But Hartman signed with the Commanders as a free agent.

Hartman was part of the active roster for the first five games of the 2024 season and the final two games of the 2025 season without appearing in a game. He also started Washington's first preseason game in August against the New England Patriots.

During the 2026 season, Hartman is Washington's fourth-string quarterback behind starter Jayden Daniels, backup Marcus Mariota and rookie third-stringer Athan Kaliakmanis.

#2843. Why the data center slowdown concern is overblown. Two stocks to buy amid the fear

The demise of data centers has been greatly exaggerated. So says every utility CEO and energy-focused investor we spoke with at the Edison Electric Institute's annual conference in Arizona. Here's why...

There is a lot of misleading, misdirected, or straight-up incorrect information floating around about data centers and the AI buildout. I'm not going to dive into the old water-use story — you can do your own research on that — but rather focus this week on what the insiders know: Some newly elected politicians either don't have the power to stop the buildouts or, if they do, the buildout will likely just continue somewhere else.

Ohio and Georgia are important states to watch with their new incoming political class, but for the sheer importance in size and scope, I'll focus on the energy and data center capital: Texas.

Incoming Governor Gina Hinojosa and incoming Senator James Talarico both campaigned on promises to go after what they believe to be affordability issues around data centers. I say "what they believe to be" because it's unclear how much data centers may raise your electricity bill, if at all.

First and foremost, data centers are largely a state issue, meaning officials elected to federal office have little control over outcomes, at least for now. In Texas, any cancellations or moratoriums on new builds will come from the statehouse in Austin. That's where current Governor Greg Abbott has already, and rather quietly, put his own "pause" on data center permitting. That delay has been underway for a few weeks. Also, in Texas, the Governor has less direct power than in some other states. The power the governor does have largely derives from the authority to appoint those in charge of the all-important permitting process.

In the Lone Star State, that power lies with two agencies: the Public Utility Commission of Texas (PUCT) and the even lesser-known Texas Commission on Environmental Quality (TCEQ). The PUCT regulates Texas' electricity, telecom, and water industries. It helps oversee growth of anything electricity-grid-related. So if a data center wants to connect to the public utility grid, it needs the commission's approval. Its chair — a pro-growth chap named Thomas Gleeson —is also on the board of Texas' electric grid powerhouse ERCOT and will serve his term with the utility commission through September 2029. Yes, three years from now.

That agency gets involved when a data center or power provider wants to connect to the electricity grid. But what about facilities that are off the grid and have their own dedicated power sources? Texas has less control over what happens then. That said, it does have to authorize certain environmental permits, and that responsibility largely lies with the TCEQ. The TCEQ was "BBI" (my own little term for "boring, but important") until data centers came along. It's not exactly the Alamo, but this obscure group — run by a regulator named Brooke Paup — is largely the "last stand" for permitting on those new builds. Paup was also appointed by Gov. Abbott and serves through next year. In other words, unless she and Gleeson step down or there is some way to oust them, the regulatory environment in Texas may not change much.

The bottom line in this brief foray into the Texas regulatory swamp is to highlight that there are things for investors to consider around the data center/energy world that may not be at the top of the headlines.

Important Note → Be mindful that I am an East Coast-based financial broadcaster and journalist, not an expert on Texas regulation or how the state operates. There may very well be things I don't know, things my sources aren't aware of, or other ways in which the new Governor and state House can assert power. For one, she recently said she may try to freeze electricity rates. Hint: that would be a terrible idea unless you want to damage the growth of badly needed new power sources like wind and solar.

Disclaimer is done. Let's assume I am wrong on Texas and there is a larger push to slow or stop data center growth in the state. Conversations with some pretty smart people lead me to believe that if those facilities aren't built in that state, they will be built in another: Louisiana, Oklahoma, or another state. Sources tell me that Louisiana Governor Jeff Landry and his team are already working the phones, telling companies that his state would love to welcome new business. Same for Oklahoma. Both are gas-rich, have plenty of power, and are only too happy to grab any data center dollars.

Let's acknowledge that not all data centers are going to be built… and never were. There are way too many plans to make them all happen. Some DCs were shelved before the election. Some others may soon be canceled, either for reasons of public pushback or otherwise. It's also not clear if we even need all the planned compute. There will be a time when companies realize they have enough — or at least enough for now — and turn off the spending taps.

Goldman Sachs lays it out in a new note late this week:

"The midterm election results pose only modest risks to AI investment, for now. We expect newly elected Democratic governors in states that account for over a third of planned US data center capacity to impose moratoria on new data centers. However, these will likely only impact projects not yet permitted, leaving the vast majority of planned 2027 construction unaffected. We therefore expect data center construction spending to remain strong next year, though moratoria that are stricter or longer-lasting than we expect pose downside risks."

The firm adds that it still sees spending on AI-related hardware coming in around $1.4 trillion by the end of next year. In other words, the spending boom is going to keep going, at least for now.

Another key point for all you energy- or utility-focused investors out there: While data centers are one huge driver of electricity demand, they aren't the only one. Electric cars, reshoring manufacturing, and the "electrification of everything" will also grow the need for more gigawatts. This is why we asked every utility CEO we spoke with at the EEI conference if they saw any slowdown in their internal electricity growth estimates. All gave the same answer: no. Not one hinted at a downturn. Not Exelon's Calvin Butler. Not Entergy's Drew Marsh. And not Drew Maloney, head of EEI. Of course, power demand is about more than just data centers, but it's still important for investors to hear it from the top.

Side note: Many of you think I'm wrong about my "hot take" on data centers and the election. If you missed last week's Power Insider, this is how you responded to my X poll on the election and energy.

So… why am I writing this week about AI and data centers? Because, as I've said before and will keep saying, AI and data centers are just energy stories in disguise. If they grow, energy demand grows. Demand for solar, wind, and natural gas grows. The economy grows. Because, after all, energy is the economy.

Oh, and in the past week, I've flown from Newark to Rome to Chicago to Phoenix and back to Newark. My brain may be a little mushy, so bear with me.

So now that my big take on data centers and regulation is over, let's get granular. What energy stocks do you buy right now?

Let's get the "1-2-3" on six stocks: one in solar, two in power, and three in oil and gas.

One: William Blair is upgrading First Solar (FSLR) to Outperform. The election result is part of the story. Analyst Jed Dorsheimer and his team believe the new Congress and certain state governorships will benefit local support for utility-scale solar, and that First Solar is best-positioned, though he does caution investors that it may take "some time to show up in the bookings." He adds that Texas is the largest builder of solar, so the shift in state-level leadership is a big deal. Fair warning, however: upgrades do not mean the stock will go higher. Deutsche Bank upgraded FSLR back in early July — an upgrade I wrote about — and the stock has fallen 22% since.

Two: Staying with the macro power theme, Barclays' Nicholas Campanella is particularly optimistic on two utility names. He tells Power Insider that he "really likes NiSource (NI) and Xcel Energy (XEL) right now." It has to do in part with what I just wrote about above. Campanella says the political outcomes in states such as Texas and Georgia may drive investors into utilities in other areas, particularly those with "more stable and established data center policies in place." That would fit with Indiana-based NiSource and Minnesota-based Xcel. While Minnesota may have a Democratic tilt, the company operates in eight different states. It is also working with Alphabet (GOOGL) on a massive new data center, and CEO Bob Frenzel told us that he is confident they can successfully navigate it all.

Three: Now to the "3"… three picks on what powers some of the power: oil and natural gas.

In an interview on CNBC, Bernstein analyst Bob Brackett says he likes ExxonMobil (XOM), calling it a great "barbell" play between natural gas and refined products. Brackett — a former Exxon employee — also believes it's a relatively "safe" stock to own right now in the volatile and headline-driven world of energy. Brackett has an Outperform rating and $186 target on the stock, giving it about another 10% upside from here.

Brackett also likes the lesser-known Expand Energy (EXE). If you're not familiar with Expand Energy, don't worry; it's still relatively new. The company is the combination of Chesapeake Energy and Southwestern Energy. It's a natural gas behemoth, and Brackett says the gas story is a "great long-term story" that's trading below a "midcycle price," and Expand fits right into it. Brackett also notes that Expand has a new CEO. He has a $139 target on the stock, making him one of the more bullish analysts on the Street. Expand has a median price target of $123.54.

Meanwhile, the chart looks tasty on another energy name: Cheniere Energy (LNG). Piper Sandler technical guru Craig Johnson likes the stock, and while he isn't a fundamental energy analyst, he was one of the few to upgrade the entire sector back in February when the Iran war began. Cheniere — along with Venture Global (VG) — is a pure play on why I was just in Europe: the worsening European energy crisis. Cheniere exports massive quantities of U.S. natural gas to customers in Europe, Asia, and other parts of the world. Johnson likes Cheniere in part because it recently broke out of a "very big base," and he wants to buy stocks that are on the upswing.

Cheniere is a well-run, rather quiet company with a fantastic CEO in longtime energy executive Jack Fusco. Jack has forgotten more about running an energy business than most will know, and the company continues to expand production. As I've pointed out for over four years, without American natural gas, Europe would be in dire straits and cutting demand even more than it already is. It's not hyperbole to say that American natural gas is — literally — keeping the lights and the heat on across Germany, Italy, and other countries.

Remember this incredible stat: just one tanker of LNG can produce a year's worth of power for over 100,000 average-sized European homes. Next year, Europe is still planning to stop buying LNG from Russia. That means demand for American natural gas is only going one way: up.

This week's Inside Line is with Amrita Sen, founder and director of market intelligence at Energy Aspects. Amrita is one of the smartest and most well-connected energy experts in the world, and she took a few minutes from her constant travel to answer a couple of questions.

This week's Take A Look is a little outside the norm, but an important interview nonetheless. It's with the U.S. Ambassador to Italy, business magnate, and former star of CNBC's Billion Dollar Buyer, Tilman Fertitta. In a wide-ranging interview from the ambassador's residence in Rome, we talked about that day's stock market crash, business, the consumer, and, of course, energy. Full disclosure: Tilman and I have been friends for over 15 years.

This Washington Post article was published a few days after the election. The underlying research from Harvard University and the University of Pennsylvania that it was based on is here and is worth a read. The study came out on October 9 (and yes, I missed it as well).

Not blaming data centers for higher energy costs is definitely random, but interesting!

#2842. Chicago Fed's Goolsbee says central bank 'has definitely got work to do' on inflation

Chicago Federal Reserve President Austan Goolsbee expressed concern about the latest inflation data, telling CNBC on Friday that the central bank likely will need to take action.

"I believe that the the central bank has definitely got work to do to continue to reestablish its credibility," the policymaker said during an interview on "Squawk Box."

His remarks came shortly after the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported that the producer price index, considered a proxy for wholesale prices, posted a 0.3% increase in October, which was slightly less than expected, but also indicated a 6.5% annual gain, the highest since November 2022.

With data in hand earlier in the week showing that the consumer price index rose a bit more than expected during the month, Goolsbee said the Fed is slipping further away from its 2% inflation target.

"We've been above 2% for almost six years, and while it sometimes we were making progress, lately we're not making any progress, and if anything, it's going the wrong way," he said.

"Inflation is rising. It's proved far more persistent than we predicted at the beginning from the supply shocks, and now we're adding this old-fashioned demand overheating coming from the AI data center buildout, that looks like it's starting to spread out of its lane," Goolsbee added.

Markets are fully pricing in a quarter percentage point increase when the rate-setting Federal Open Market Committee meets next on Dec. 8-9. There also is a better than 10% probability of a half-point hike, according to the CME Group's FedWatch gauge of futures markets. Goolsbee is not currently an FOMC voter but will be in 2027.

Earlier in the week, Fed Governor Christopher Waller indicated he would entertain a 50 basis point move depending on this week's inflation data.

Goolsbee did not speculate directly on whether the Fed might need to be more aggressive.

"It's it's all going to be about this ... question: What is happening to inflation, and are we on path back to 2%?" he said. "My feeling is, right now we're not."

#2841. Joel Embiid’s aggressiveness was a welcome sight for Sixers

The Sixers losing two out of three games wasn't necessarily cause for much concern, but the way they lost those games raised some alarm bells. Particularly with Joel Embiid, who has yet to find his shot this season. His defense also began to deteriorate over the past couple of games as he dealt with plantar fasciitis in his right foot.

Through his first 10 games of the season, Embiid shot just 37% from the field and a career-low 55% from the rim. That included a game where he went 0-for-9 from the field in Cleveland against the Cavaliers, one of the worst performances of his career.

After being blown out by the Boston Celtics, Embiid admitted that he might be in his own head.

"[I'm] probably overthinking it too much," he said, "and then I think the rhythm of the game is different. I'm so used to having to create for myself, so it's a little bit different."

The Toronto Raptors coming to town provided a good opportunity for a get-right game. Not only were they down multiple key starters and on the second night of a back-to-back, but their starting center, Jakob Poeltl, has struggled mightily all season.

Embiid didn't completely snap out of his funk. His 20 points came on 7-of-17 shooting. Toronto was able to get into the lane with relative ease all night, but anyone watching the game could tell that Embiid was moving around the floor much better than he had in recent outings as the Sixers got back in the win column. That fact was capped off by Embiid dunking not once but twice.

"I think it's a really, really big positive," said head coach Nick Nurse. "I think that he's just moving better, and I'm not sure that was the case for a couple games here recently."

Embiid was able to do all of this after scaring every Sixers fan at the beginning of the game. Early in the first quarter he bumped knees with Ja'Kobe Walter as he was setting a screen and went down in a heap of pain. He went back to the locker room but was able to return three minutes later of game time.

Nurse said that Embiid was "very, very motivated" to play once he had returned to the locker room — so much so that Nurse had trouble subbing him out for his scheduled rests. Nurse did say that's a good problem, sometimes.

Embiid didn't talk after the game, but he told reporters at shootaround before the game that he needed to be more aggressive. While he still didn't hit as many midrange jumpers as he's used to, that aggression was visible in his shot selection — 10 of his 17 attempts came from the paint and it looked like he really started to hunt dunks. He nearly had another self-lob off the backboard, but Collin Murray-Boyles got in the way, forcing Embiid to kick the ball out to Kentavious Caldwell-Pope for a three.

"It's nice to see the kind of movement spark come back," Nurse said. "You've heard me say this a lot too. When he's driving the ball, he's feeling pretty good."

There's still a ways to go for Embiid getting back on track. That lethal jumper he's so famous for still has to come around. At the same time, the aggression he showed in attacking the basket was a welcome sight for every Sixer.

"I always tell him, 'Bro, like you're the MVP of this league,'" VJ Edgecombe said, "you're that good, you're that good.' I always tell him, 'No one can stop you. There's not one person in the NBA than can guard you.'"

If this is the start of Embiid putting it together, the Sixers' offense can finally start to look as dangerous as it was advertised over the summer. The fact that they've started the season 10-3 in the mean time is one hell of a cushion.

#2839. Cal, Stanford and San Jose State all playing to keep college football bowl bids alive

As Cal football (3-6, 2-4 ACC) looks to turn its disappointing season around, general manager Ron Rivera and head coach Tosh Lupoi are preaching "growth and development" in their approach to the final three games of the season ahead of their game against Virginia.

"I'd say the biggest thing is we can't control what's behind us, and, yeah, it has been disappointing," Rivera said with Lupoi seated rnext to him at the team's weekly Tuesday press conference. "We have an opportunity to go out and do some really special things. We've got three games left. They're all against ACC opponents, and I think the work and the development and growth is what we're looking at."

Lupoi added, "I would just gauge it where we're at too. Just the pulse of the team and the way these guys are fighting and excited and really attacking every day," echoing Rivera.

While Lupoi and Rivera made an effort to display unity, it was hard to ignore turmoil within the program. During the Bears' bye week, Rivera went on KNBR's "Murph & Markus" show and addressed some of the growing pains Lupoi has dealt with in his first season as a head coach, saying he's "bumping his head" while he also pointed to the failures of inexperienced coordinators brought in and a lack of receptiveness to some of the advice he has given him.

While Rivera acknowledged that's to be expected with a new head coach, he also made it clear the clock is ticking, saying: "Now it's time. These last three games, hopefully four, the trajectory has to be going north. We have to show that we are growing."

Adding to the tension, The Athletic published an article detailing "chaos" behind the scenes and the growing rift between Lupoi and Rivera. To that you can add in the statistical indicators of bad football, with Cal ranking 129th in yards per play (4.5), 115th in yards allowed per play (6.1), 125th in points per game (18.5), and 122nd in points allowed per game (34.6), tied for dead last in penalties per game (9.1).

Hence the need for public unity, with Rivera and Lupoi emphasizing "that's behind us" as they fielded questions about the program's shortcomings.

Asked if the characterization of the program as "mired in chaos" was fair, Lupoi responded, "I have no idea what the reference was, but certainly I produce chaos every day at practice, so not sure if it's that."

One big question regarding the future is about quarterback Jaron-Keawe Sagapolutele.

"Do you expect him to return next season?" a reporter asked Rivera and Lupoi, amid what has proven to be a borderline nightmarish sophomore season for the young QB.

"I believe he can come back," Rivera said. "We'll see what happens."

The next three weeks will determine the direction of Cal football, starting with a must-win game against Virginia.

When Cal has the ball: The Cavaliers will be tough for Sagapolutele and the struggling offense, as they have a strong pass defense. They've held opposing quarterbacks to just 192.4 yards per game this season and rank 17th in the country in opposing passing success rate (38.6%).

"They're seventh in the country and generating pressures, getting after the quarterback, a lot of stunts up front," Lupoi noted.

With No. 1 running back Adam Mohammed off the injury report after missing four games this season, Cal will look to rebalance its attack by establishing the run against a Virginia defense that has allowed 173.6 rushing yards per game.

When Virginia has the ball: While Cal's offense hasn't been good, Virginia's no better. The Cavaliers have mustered an average of just 12 points per game over their last three, scoring three touchdowns total in that stretch.

Senior QB Beau Pribula has been underwhelming, and running back Jekeil Middlebrook and the ground game have been far from successful, ranking 114th in expected points added (EPA) per rush (-0.05). If there was a get-right game for the Bears' defense after getting blasted for 131 total points over their last three games, this would be it. The challenge for Cal will be keeping Virginia's rushing attack bottled up.

"It's a dual-threat quarterback. Beau, No. 7, does a great job running that offense," Lupoi emphasized. "They've got the most attempted runs in the ACC. They want to run the rock."

Notable injuries: Cal is close to full strength, with only wide receiver Jordan King joining running back Carter Vargas, tight end Dorian Thomas and defensive lineman Nate Burrell among unavailable Bears. Virginia's injury report is long, but the most notable name is that of linebacker and leading tackler Maddox Marcellus, listed as doubtful.

Key stats to remember: The Cal offense ranks 125th in the country on both early downs EPA (-0.07) and 3rd/4th down success rate (35.8%), a bad recipe. Virginia is one of the least penalized teams, with just 5.0 a game (tied for 21st). The difference in penalty yards per game between the two teams is 35.8 yards, longer than any rushing attempt Cal has had so far this season.

Stanford quarterback Davis Warren (8) in action during a game against Louisville in Louisville, Ky., Saturday, Oct. 31, 2026. (Timothy D. Easley/Associated Press)

After allowing four second-half touchdowns in a loss to Louisville before its bye, Stanford (4-5, 2-4 ACC) looks to get back on track against the Virginia Tech Hokies (6-3, 3-3 ACC).

"Coming out of that Louisville game, we got to figure out how to play four quarters, or more, of winning football," head coach Tavita Pritchard told reporters Tuesday. "That's the step that we have to take, that's the gap we have to close here, and that's the space that we're in now."

Pritchard's squad needs to win two out of their final three games to qualify for their first bowl game since 2018. The Cardinal have a difficult but winnable opportunity in Virginia Tech to get one step closer to what would be a sizable success for the first-year coach.

Senior quarterback Davis Warren will try to add to his strong season against a Hokies defense that has struggled against the pass. Virginia Tech has surrendered 250.9 passing yards per game while ranking 100th in opposing passing success rate (46.5%).

Ideally, the Cardinal will establish the run and play the "complementary football" Pritchard has been pushing the team to achieve. That will be difficult against the Hokies, who rank a respectable 55th in opposing EPA per rush (0.01).

With Warren up to 2,203 yards and 14 passing touchdowns, the formula for Stanford will be to keep him clean in the pocket and lean on his arm. But that will be a tall task as the Hokies lead the ACC with 28 sacks.

When Virginia Tech has the ball: Stanford's defense has struggled, and will have its hands full facing QB Ethan Grunkenmeyer, whose 2,431 passing yards ranks 14th in the country. With the Cardinal surrendering 280.1 yards in the air to opposing quarterbacks (130th), the Hokies are expected to have an easy time pushing the ball downfield and cutting up the Cardinal secondary.

Virginia Tech's ground game also has a great opportunity to run over Stanford, which has allowed 197.1 rushing yards a game (123rd) and ranks third-to-last in opposing EPA per rush (0.34). Sophomore running back Jeffrey Overton Jr. is fresh off a 123-yard rushing performance, his third 100-yard rushing game of the season.

Notable injuries: Virginia Tech's starting left tackle Justin Terry is listed as probable to return, which would boost their heft in the trenches.

Key stats to remember: Stanford's defensive success rate ranks second to last in the country, with teams creating a "successful play" to stay ahead of the chains relative to down-and-distance 51.1% of the time.

San José State Spartans running back Jabari Bates runs during a game against the New Mexico Lobos in San Jose, Saturday, Oct. 31, 2026. (Scot Tucker/Associated Press)

San Jose State (5–5, 3–3 Mountain West) heads to Air Force (7–2, 3–2 Mountain West), which is coming off reclaiming the Commander-in-Chief's Trophy against Army for the first time since 2022. The Spartans need to win two of their final three games to qualify for their second bowl game under head coach Ken Niumatalolo.

"We know we have our work cut out for us," Niumatalolo told reporters Monday. "We have to play our best to have a chance to beat these guys, especially there; it's a hard place to play. Good team, good football coaches, good environment, hard-nosed kids, but we're looking forward to it."

When SJSU has the ball: Freshman QB Daniel Rolovich will look to build off completing 16 of his 27 attempts for 231 yards and a touchdown against Northern Illinois last week.

"I thought Daniel, in his second full start, led us to victory again," Niumatalolo said. "Encouraged by that, you got a true freshman who helped us win."

Rolovich is in for a taller task than the winless Huskies he beat last week. With the Falcons limiting opponents to just 319.5 yards per game, the Spartans will need more from their freshman quarterback to overcome one of the conference's tougher defenses.

When Air Force has the ball: The Falcons wear down opponents with their ground attack, ranking first in the country with 319.6 rushing yards a game, and seventh in EPA per rush (0.19). They have six ball carriers with 60+ rushes and 300+ yards. The Spartans allow a respectable 158.1 ground yards a game.

Notable injuries: No key injuries for either team.

Key stats to remember: San Jose State rank 121st in opposing rushing success rate (48.7%), while Air Force quite literally outruns opponents, with their 319.6 rushing yards per game being just barely ahead of the 319.5 total yards they allow.

This article originally published at Cal, Stanford and San Jose State all playing to keep college football bowl bids alive.

#2838. Steelers DL Derrick Harmon questionable for Bengals matchup

The Pittsburgh Steelers are coming out of their Week 9 bye week at 6-2 overall and will battle for sole possession of first place in the AFC North on Sunday Night Football against the Cincinnati Bengals. Heading into the contest, Pittsburgh is in a good place health-wise.

After logging two limited practices to open the week and a full practice in Friday's walkthrough, defensive tackle Derrick Harmon is listed as questionable to play on Sunday night. #2837. Hackers stole $6.3 billion from Binance—2 habits that take 'a few seconds' can help you avoid similar traps

The biggest heist in cryptocurrency history began with small mistakes many internet users make. A hacking group tied to North Korea recently stole $6.3 billion from the crypto exchange Binance. The operation was sophisticated, involving fake job interviews and malicious code.

But it was also mundane. Although artificial intelligence played a role in the hack, this was not a cyberattack of the future, propelled by swarms of AI agents. Rather, it relied on humans who made some basic errors, like clicking a link because it appeared first on a search results page or trusting strangers at a fake company without taking a few seconds to look them up.

We work at the Digital Inquiry Group, a nonprofit that researches how people evaluate online information and creates free, evidence-based curriculum that helps people decide what to trust.

In over a decade of research, we've found that people frequently engage in online behavior that makes them more likely to get duped. Two simple strategies can help you better navigate today's information landscape, from traditional phishing to misleading social posts to AI fakes. Here's how.

The Binance heist began when one of the company's engineers downloaded compromised software. That engineer, according to the Reuters investigation of the hack, clicked on a sponsored search result that appeared higher on the results page and directed them to the malicious code.

Our research at the Digital Inquiry Group suggests internet users frequently behave as if first is best. They click on the first thing they see. They incorrectly associate a higher placement with vetted content, ceding judgment to tech company algorithms. Students have gone so far as to tell us "Google is almost never wrong" or "AI is always right."

Professional fact-checkers commonly practice click restraint. They understand that whether you're searching on the internet or querying a chatbot, first isn't always best. If you're researching an organization, the first link will often be to its own site, which is hardly likely to disclose anything that's wrong with it.

So the next time you search for something online, be wary of links to sponsored content, the organization you're investigating, or to open platforms where anyone can post and information isn't verified, such as Quora or Reddit. Read the brief snippets beneath each link, and make a more informed choice about what to open.

Always ask yourself: Who is behind this information? What makes them credible? Look for people and organizations that have direct expertise on the topic at hand, or have processes to ensure the accuracy of information, such as news outlets that employ editors and fact-checkers.

Binance says it caught that first malicious download, replaced the device and changed passwords. But later, another engineer downloaded compromised code as part of what they thought was a job interview, which included a virtual meeting with the supposed CEO of a fake company.

It's tempting to think that the engineer should have seen something was off. But the internet is a precarious place because bad actors know how to make content feel legitimate. Binance says its employee was snared by a hacker posing as an HR rep who "shared a social media account with him, including personal photos and videos posted on the platform, in order to build trust."

When they encounter a piece of unfamiliar content, professional fact-checkers don't just read it closely — they read laterally. They open new browser tabs to see what other, credible sources have to say. Fact-checkers know that bad actors make themselves look good on their home turf, but that it's a lot harder to fake a good reputation across the internet.

The next time you encounter something unfamiliar online, look for authoritative sources that can shed light on the person or organization. You might find a news article that confirms the claim you're looking into, or a report that identifies a supposedly neutral source as biased.

In some cases, you may find nothing at all, which is a signal in itself: if you look up a company that reached out to you and find no external confirmation of its existence, that should be an immediate red flag.

Had that first Binance engineer engaged in click restraint and verified the source of the software, they might have avoided the malicious download. Had that second employee taken time to seek external verification about the supposed HR rep, rather than trusting a polished social media profile, they might have realized that something didn't add up.

The internet and AI have made it harder to know whether what we see is authentic. They've also made it easier than ever before to investigate who or what we encounter online. It's always worth pausing to check. A few seconds up front can save us all a lot of time, grief and money later on.

Nadav Ziv is a researcher at the Digital Inquiry Group. Joel Breakstone is co-founder and executive director of the Digital Inquiry Group.

#2836. The Grizzlies are way ahead of schedule, but still have a long way to go

Every season comes with its own unique twists and turns. If it wasn't that way, all of our offseason prediction pieces would be perfect and there would be no point in playing any games.

Fortunately, this year has been no different. Chief among all the unexpected outcomes are the Memphis Grizzlies, who, in what was supposed to be the first year of a rebuild, sit at 7-4 with the 10th-best net-rating in the entire association.

With teams like the Oklahoma City Thunder and Minnesota Timberwolves enduring significant injuries, and others like the Denver Nuggets looking too old for their own good, it is plausible that the West is more wide-open than we initially believed. But is it possible for the Grizzlies to be this ahead of schedule? Or is this just a flash of what they will eventually become?

It feels cliche to say this given how much this team has permeated the NBA zeitgeist since they won it all, but this Grizzlies group really does resemble the 2013-14 Beautiful Game San Antonio Spurs.

The Spurs were an incredibly deep team that reached the Platonic ideals for decision-making speed, spacing, and ball movement. Head coach Tuomas Iisalo has the Grizzlies on this current trajectory.

Iisalo basically load manages his team to ensure that they are as fresh as possible. This year, 13 Grizzlies have played at least 70 total minutes. The only other teams that can say that are the Indiana Pacers, Golden State Warriors, Oklahoma City Thunder, and Utah Jazz  – all of whom have had to deal with their fair share of injuries. So, their digging deep into their bench is more out of necessity than it is a tactical decision.

Under this new regime, the Grizzlies are deploying a multi-layered, ball-screen continuity attack predicated around the pick-and-roll and side-to-side ball movement. Iisalo quite literally refers to the concept as "flow," which is poetic considering the way the offense softly cascades from action to action. The intricacies of their futuristic style are outside of the scope of the current discussion, but if you want to learn more, be sure to check out this deep dive from Coach Gibson Pyper:

As any coach will tell you, strategy will only take you so far. You need the players who can execute your vision or else it doesn't matter how smart you are. This offense requires skilled operates (Ty Jerome and Cam Spencer), strong screeners (Zach Edey, Cam Boozer, and Isaiah Stewart), shooters/closeout attackers (Cedric Coward, Jaylen Wells, Jerami Grant, and Quentin Post), and speedy court mappers (Boozer may be better than any rookie at this in all of NBA history).

The bottom line is that this team has a litany of legit players. This also shows on the defensive side of the ball, where the Grizzlies currently rank fifth (just like on offense). It starts in the middle. Prior to the start of the season, I called Stewart one of the best defensive bigs in the league, and the only thing stopping me from doing the same with Edey were his lingering injury concerns. Edey, in particular, is looking like a fringe Defensive Player of the Year candidate. When he's out there, the Grizzlies are in the 99th percentile in defensive rating (per Cleaning the Glass). That's in a league that features Victor Wembanyama. Between him and Stewart, that's an excellent defensive foundation.

Wells and Coward are a pair of rangy wings, with powers that perfectly compliment each other (Wells is better at defending the ball, Coward has more of a presence off-ball). Grant and GG Jackson have size and length. Boozer moves better than anyone with his muscle mass should reasonably be expected to. Kris Murray would get more buzz for his defensive prowess if his offense wasn't so damaging.

Iisalo's theory about keeping bodies fresh has come to fruition. While many teams are operating at half-speed, conserving themselves for the 82-game grind, the Grizzlies are moving like it's the first round of the postseason. An abundance of youthful vigor (the Grizzlies are the third-youngest team in the league) certainly helps, too. Look at the way Wells and Boozer are sliding their feet in this first clip, or the diligence from Spencer to x-out and kill the advantage created by Braden Smith:

If you're a fan of the team or in any way affiliated with the organization, you should feel very excited about what you are building. Boozer, Coward, Edey, Wells, Jackson, and Stewart are all 25 years old and younger. The future is bright in Memphis, but for right now, there are some indicators that tell us negative regression is coming.

The Grizzlies are second in 3-point percentage and eighth opponent 3-point percentage. Opponent 3-point percentage is almost exclusively contingent on variables outside of a defense's control, and, on the flip side, I doubt this group will remain the second-best shooting team in the association for the entirety of the marathon.

Dunks & Threes has a metric which ranks a team's strength of schedule from the games they've already played (SOS). According to that, the Grizzlies have had the second-easiest schedule this year, with their best win coming against a Warriors team that had yet to hit its stride.

In their recent loss against the Indiana Pacers, we got front row seats at their biggest shortfall: guard play/ball handling. Outside of Ty Jerome, Cam Spencer, and Scottie Pippen Jr. (who is hurt right now), the Grizzlies don't have much in terms of pressure breakers, and none of those three gentlemen is a black belt in this art form. The Pacers' token fullcourt pressure hounded the Grizzlies all night, to the tune of 20 turnovers and their lowest offensive rating output of the season.

This could be remedied if Iisalo started playing two of these three together more frequently, but in that case, it would weaken their formidability on the other side of the ball. Patching up one whole leads to another. A telltale sign of a flawed unit.

If I was a prospective general manager, who could pick a ten-year gig with any team in the league, it would take one hand to count off the places I'd pick over the Grizzlies. What they have here is real, and it seems more bulletproof than the last generation (no pun intended, I swear). But right now, in 2026-27, I find it difficult to see the Grizzlies as anything more than a fringe playoff team.

#2835. OpenAI launches ChatGPT Wallet, allowing its 'dots' AI agents to make purchases for users

OpenAI on Friday announced ChatGPT Wallet, a new payment feature that enables its "dots" artificial intelligence agents to make purchases on behalf of users.

The company built ChatGPT Wallet in partnership with American Express, Mastercard and Visa, and it allows cardholders to maintain their existing credit card rewards and protections. Eligible users enter their card information once, prompt their dot to find items to buy and approve each purchase with a security check.

OpenAI said its dots agents will never actually access a user's card details. Instead, their payment networks will provide the dot with a "payment token," which contains a scoped card number, security code and expiration date that is specific to a given transaction.

"Our goal here is to really focus on merchants and our network partners and start building out a really great foundation that's safe and easy to use," Chaya Nayak, who leads product for monetization and payments at OpenAI, told CNBC in an interview.

ChatGPT Wallet comes as a growing number of consumers are exploring how to use personal AI agents to manage their calendars, book travel and complete other day-to-day tasks. OpenAI introduced the dots agents during its annual developer conference in late September, shortly after Meta's Muse offering topped the App Store charts.

Purchases made through ChatGPT Wallet could eventually kick-start a new revenue stream for OpenAI, but the company said it is not taking a cut of transactions to start. With the initial launch, OpenAI is working to build relationships with merchants and position itself as a serious platform for shoppers, especially as vendors are beginning to experiment with personal agents.

OpenAI has pushed deeper into AI-enabled commerce over the last year, but some of its early attempts failed to take off. The company introduced an "Instant Checkout" feature last fall, which it quickly pivoted away from. By March, it had rolled out a revamped shopping experience that aimed to make it easier for users to find and compare products.

Nayak said ChatGPT Wallet builds on OpenAI's previous efforts, particularly around product discovery.

"We think that this is the next step, where if someone found something they're really excited to buy, then we want to be able to get them through to that endpoint," she said.

OpenAI's ChatGPT chatbot supports more than 1.2 billion weekly active users and nearly 100 million paying users as of the end of the third quarter. With such a broad user base, Gebb said OpenAI can decide to "turn up the volume" on ChatGPT Wallet when it wants to.

"It just makes sense to watch it, start it in a contained environment, and then keep rolling from there," Gebb told CNBC in an interview.

Merchants and vendors are still reckoning with the rise of personal AI agents, and some companies have opted to forgo them entirely. United Airlines said Wednesday that it will block agents from accessing its systems to book tickets, and Amazon prevents the agents from purchasing products on its platform.

OpenAI said Friday that agentic commerce is still in its "early days," and it published tools and guidance alongside its ChatGPT Wallet announcement to help businesses start to navigate the transition. The company explained how merchants can make sure their websites aren't inadvertently blocking OpenAI's agents, for instance.

"It's going to take time for the ecosystem to build the right infrastructure to be able to make this experience really amazing and seamless across all of their sites," Nayak said. "Really, who we are speaking to here is the networks and the merchants."

#2834. Fantasy football Week 10 streamers: Who can save your lineup from bye weeks?

Post-NFL trade deadline, four teams on bye weeks, and the push for the fantasy playoffs is upon us.

Let's look at 10 players who are widely available and who you can stream in Week 10 if your lineup is in need of help.

(Streamers are players who are rostered in 50% or fewer of Yahoo leagues.)

Among quarterbacks with at least 50 dropbacks, Lock leads the league with 0.33 EPA/play. Of course, it's a much smaller sample size for him overall, but he's performing well so far, and that should continue against the Las Vegas Raiders. In three games this season (two starts), he's posted 12.7, 21.4 and 12.6 fantasy points. They're not massive numbers, but with the Raiders allowing the third-most fantasy points to quarterbacks (20.4), Lock is in another good spot.

With an over/under total of 49.5 points, we have to consider options from this game for streaming. The Steelers are coming off their bye week and face a Bengals defense that has allowed 17.3 points per game to quarterbacks. Rodgers put that on full display in Week 3 when he posted 23.68 fantasy points with 292 yards, three touchdowns and one interception, which was a huge showing. That's certainly his ceiling, but seeing 14-18 points from Rodgers as a streamer is very viable at this point in the season.

Estime played on 47% of the snaps last week compared to 49% for Alvin Kamara, but Estime is the option you want. Kamara only posted 7.9 PPR points on nine touches, while Estime posted 14.7 on 16 touches. Estime out-carried Kamara 15-6. It's an elite matchup for Estime, since the Carolina Panthers have allowed a whopping 27.6 PPR fantasy points to running backs, which is the second-most in the NFL. With D'Andre Swift, Saquon Barkley, Bucky Irving and Jonah Coleman all on bye weeks, Estime is a solid streaming option this week.

Johnson stepped in last week to lead the team with 14 carries for 68 yards and a touchdown after Jacobs went out with a finger injury. While MarShawn Lloyd is expected back this week, he's been largely ineffective overall, posting more than 10.0 PPR points just one time this season. Johnson doesn't have any pass-catching upside, but in a huge divisional matchup, seeing the Packers commit to the run game against the Vikings' defense shouldn't come as a shock. We saw James Cook on the Bills have success last week, providing a blueprint for the Packers.

With George Pickens still listed as questionable, Flournoy could be in for another starting role opposite CeeDee Lamb. While Flournoy had that role last week and ended with only 7.3 PPR points, he played on a season-high 97% of the offensive snaps and ran 38 routes, which was tied with Lamb. It's certainly a tougher matchup against the San Francisco 49ers' defense, but the role and playing time are there, which can't be said about all streamers in the free agent pool.

I know the Chargers don't inspire any confidence, but with four teams on bye weeks there are several normal starting wide receivers that aren't available this week. Ladd McConkey wasn't able to go last week and it allowed Johnston to post his best game of the season with 12.80 PPR points. The Ravens allow the 11th-most PPR points per game (34.0) to wide receivers, making this a decent matchup for Johnston if your league is deep and you are desperate for a starter.

Matthew Stafford has to throw the ball to someone, right? Davante Adams is still out, and Puka Nacua is now out. That makes Atwell and Konata Mumpfield the two starting wide receivers. Some people might have forgotten that the Rams reacquired Atwell at the end of August after he signed with the Miami Dolphins during the offseason. Last week, Atwell played on 47% of the snaps, ran 25 routes and saw five targets. Those numbers don't jump off the page, but Nacua did play last week. Atwell has some experience with Stafford, and this matchup against the Arizona Cardinals is too good to pass up.

Waller has been an absolute roller coaster of fantasy production this season. There's no other way to put it. In eight games, he has had four with 10 PPR points or more and four with 6 PPR points or fewer. Total boom or bust, but sometimes you need to roll the dice and shoot for upside. His 12.8% red zone target share makes him a viable touchdown option every week, even though he might not rack up a ton of yards.

That might play into the hands of McCord, as he will look to get the ball out quickly and not take deep seven-step drops with his pass attempts.

Call me crazy, but the Bills' defense has gotten much better as the season has gone on. Since Week 5, the Bills' defense sits at -0.093 EPA/play, which is seventh in the NFL. The matchup is good against the New York Jets' offense, which is only scoring 20.2 points per game, eighth-lowest in the league. The Bills have a 20.1% pressure rate, which is the fourth-highest in the league, presenting a nice bit of upside against Geno Smith, who has taken 28 sacks this season, which is the third-most in the NFL.

#2832. They train people to avoid 'honeypot' scams—here are the common red flags they spot: 'It feels so good to be wanted'

Shawnee Delaney often kicks off a training session by telling a room full of corporate workers: "OK, raise your hand if you've ever gotten a flattering message on LinkedIn just complimenting your background and your profile and your experience."

"Every hand goes up," she says. Then, she tells attendees how responding to a simple adulatory message could unwittingly open the door to a potential romance scam.

Delaney is a corporate security expert, well-versed in teaching people how to avoid “honeypot” schemes, in which a bad actor uses romantic or sexual interest to build trust and gain information, access or leverage over a target. The term is currently making headlines in the wake of former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's resignation from the company, following reporting on his admitted affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

Honeypots, also known as "honey traps," are a very human form of social engineering attack, a category that also includes various forms of phishing and use of deepfake technology. And you don't have to be a billionaire tech CEO to possess information that scammers may value.

As competition in the global artificial intelligence race heats up, OpenAI is reportedly training employees to recognize espionage tactics including bribery, romantic manipulation and blackmail, according to The Wall Street Journal. On Nov. 3, cryptocurrency exchange Binance revealed that a recent cyberattack — which saw roughly $6 billion of digital assets stolen — was partially made possible by a fake job interview scheme that infected an employee’s work computer with malware.

"People can get solicited, bribed, romanced, etc.," says Kevin Tian, co-founder and CEO of Doppel, an AI-powered platform meant to help identify and deflect social engineering attacks. He adds: "If you've got access to sensitive IP [intellectual property] — it's not just AI companies — you can be a target."

Increasingly, scammers can use AI to scour websites ranging from Reddit to Pinterest for an individual's personal information and customize a ploy designed specifically for them, Tian says. Once they have relevant data points, attackers tend to use "the oldest tricks in the book: elicitation, flattery, ego," says Delaney, a former intelligence officer with the Defense Intelligence Agency and the founder and CEO of Washington, D.C.-based corporate security consulting firm Vaillance Group.

In many cases, Delaney notes, targets have an inkling that something is suspicious — but they'd rather allow themselves to believe the interest is legitimate. So how do you keep yourself from falling victim? Trust your self-awareness, common sense and ability to identify situations that feel too good to be true, she says.

"The bottom line is, we're all human," says Delaney, adding: "It feels so good to be wanted, or to be respected, or to be appreciated, or loved."

Delaney, who has worked with Fortune 500 companies such as Uber and Merck, advises corporate clients on cybersecurity risks and develops training programs to improve employees' awareness of security threats. Her sessions aim to build awareness of manipulative connections, such as romance scams, from how they begin to the natural progression they often take. "Introduction, then repeated contact, favors, sensitive requests — the secrecy, the pressure," she says.

Bad actors may tap into any information you post online to find an interest that can be mirrored and exploited, or an acquaintance who can be infiltrated and make an introduction. If someone you know and trust makes an introduction, you might be less likely to "go do due diligence" on the new person, even if they're a stranger, Delaney says.

One of Vaillance's services includes building a "targeting package" around an executive or C-Suite member, says Delaney. "We can show how they are vulnerable and how we would target that organization just by leveraging the people around them in that circle: spouses, friends, kids, parents, administrative assistants, neighbors," she says. "That's when people's eyes open."

From there, she discusses red flags to spot. Conversations that begin with flattery and seemingly innocuous small talk can gradually shift toward digging for information, introductions to colleagues or access to documents, says Delaney. It's human nature to want to be helpful and fulfill friendly requests, she adds. Add a potential romantic connection to the mix, especially amid an ongoing "loneliness epidemic," and the urge to comply can be even harder to resist, she says.

If you feel yourself getting cornered in a situation where a recent acquaintance or romantic partner keeps asking for information you're not comfortable revealing, Delaney says it helps to have first practiced polite refusal. "One of my favorites is: They could just respond with: 'Why do you ask?'" she says. "Put the other person on the defensive. You could give a nondescript answer. You can say you don't know. You can say you can't respond."

If you're ever suspicious of a new connection — even if you don't want to be — do yourself a favor and conduct some basic vetting, she recommends. You might examine their social media profiles and other publicly available information, for example, to see if their story checks out.

If you can't find enough information to allay your suspicions, don't be shy about alerting your employer's security or human resources team, she says. For smaller companies, or scams outside the workplace, you can file a complaint for cyber threats or romance scams with the Federal Bureau of Investigation's Internet Crime Complaint Center.

And pay attention if your new paramour starts asking you specific questions about your finances or work credentials, says Delaney.

"If you have no game at home, and you get off an airplane somewhere and suddenly everyone's all over you, we call that a red flag," she says.

#2830. Supreme Court agrees to hear Robinhood challenge to IPO information omission lawsuit

The Supreme Court on Friday agreed to hear a case in which Robinhood seeks to block a class-action lawsuit over the omission of interim financial data from the online broker's initial public offering in 2021.

Robinhood wants the Supreme Court to overturn a ruling by the 9th Circuit U.S. Court of Appeals that revived a lawsuit by investors against the company and firms involved with the IPO, including Goldman Sachs and J.P. Morgan Securities.

A San Francisco federal court judge had previously dismissed the lawsuit.

The Trump administration and the Securities and Exchange Commission had urged the Supreme Court to hear Robinhood's appeal.

"We are pleased the Supreme Court has agreed to hear our case and look forward to presenting our argument," a Robinhood spokesperson said in a statement.

CNBC has requested comment from the attorneys representing the plaintiffs in the case.

Robinhood's IPO came on the heels of a surge in investor interest during the Covid-19 pandemic in so-called meme stocks, and trading in the crypto asset dogecoin.

In July 2021, Robinhood conducted its IPO, "for which it prepared a registration statement" that "contained only limited information about Robinhood's performance during the second quarter of 2021," the 9th Circuit panel that heard the case noted.

"After the initial public offering, Robinhood reported financial results from that second quarter, and its stock price dropped," the panel wrote.

The investors who sued Robinhood and the other defendants alleged the registration statement omitted material information it was legally required to disclose.
