#2829. Wholesale prices rose 0.3% in October, putting annual rate at 6.5%, the highest in nearly four years

Wholesale prices increased less than expected in October, but inflation pressures persisted and the Federal Reserve is likely still on target for an interest rate increase soon.

The producer price index, which measures the prices domestic producers get for their products before they reach the consumer, rose a seasonally adjusted 0.3% for the month, against the Dow Jones consensus for a 0.4% increase, the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported Friday.

Stripping out food and energy, core PPI posted a 0.3% gain, meeting expectations.

On an annual basis, headline PPI accelerated 6.5%, up 0.2 percentage point for the highest annual rate since November 2022.

The report comes just a few weeks ahead of the Federal Reserve's next policy meeting, Dec. 8-9. Markets are fully pricing in a quarter percentage point increase in the central bank's key overnight borrowing rate, with slight odds that the Federal Open Market Committee might opt for a half-point hike.

Stock market futures added to gains following the release while Treasury yields were mixed.

Earlier this week, the BLS reported that inflation at the cash register was hotter than expected.

The headline consumer price index saw a 0.4% increase in October while core was up 0.3%, both 0.1 percentage point higher than expected. Because of the government shutdown in October 2025, the BLS couldn't collect some data and refrained from putting out a 12-month inflation figure for CPI.

However, the figures showed that the Fed remains a good distance from its 2% inflation goal.

"Today's PPI data – which shows some signs of improving, albeit still too-high – is unlikely to change the Fed's approach in December because the CPI data was already outside their comfort zone," said Chris Zaccarelli, chief investment officer for Northlight Asset Management.

Moreover, policymakers are beginning to worry that inflation generated by the Iran war and higher fuel prices, along with President Donald Trump's tariffs, are beginning to spread through the economy. The Fed generally would look through such price pressures as temporary, but the breadth of inflation could indicate that inflation is becoming more entrenched.

"We've had some signs of trouble now for several months in a row," Chicago Fed President Austan Goolsbee said in a CNBC interview Friday. "If you look at how broad-based the inflation is, it's not just that we're above the target and have been for some time. It's that in categories that are not driven by oil prices and not driven by tariffs, you're still seeing stalled progress and in some cases going the wrong way."

Stripping out food, energy and trade services, PPI saw an increase of 0.5% in October.

Goods prices increased 0.5% in October, with more than 60% of that move driven by goods excluding food and energy, which actually saw a 0.1% decrease. On an annual basis, the goods index jumped 10.2%, the biggest 12-month surge since October 2022.

Final demand services prices increased 0.2%, most of which was linked to transportation and warehousing services, and within that category a 3.9% gain in truck transportation of freight.

"We've been above 2% for almost six years, and while it sometimes we were making progress, lately we're not making any progress, and if anything, it's going the wrong way," Goolsbee said. "Inflation is rising; it's proved far more persistent than we predicted at the beginning from the supply shocks."

Other items in the PPI report also showed multiyear gains. Prices for intermediate processed goods soared 14.6% from October 2025, the biggest annual gain since July 2022. Intermediate prices for services posted a 6.9% increase, the most since November 2022.

#2826. After Jensen Huang’s exit, what top financial advisors are doing with Nvidia: Exclusive CNBC poll

Shares of Nvidia continued to slide after the market open on Friday after declining 4% on Thursday following news of co-founder Jensen Huang's resignation as chief executive, amid reports about his personal relationships and national security concerns. The news has raised questions for investors about what comes next for a company at the center of the artificial intelligence boom.

To gauge how wealth managers are responding, CNBC polled members of its Financial Advisor 100 and Financial Advisor Council. Thirty of the 122 firms and professionals contacted responded to the online, multiple-choice flash poll, conducted between 11 a.m. and 5 p.m. EST on Nov. 12. The poll is a snapshot of respondents' views, not a measure of all financial advisors.

Most respondents, 19, said they are holding their Nvidia positions steady. Another four said they are increasing their stake, while seven said they do not own the stock. None said they were selling at the time of the poll.

"Most of our clients are in a position to hold; the reason is because it makes up a smaller percentage of an overall diversified portfolio," Winnie Sun, managing director and founding partner of Irvine, California-based Sun Group Wealth Partners, said in an interview. Sun is a member of the CNBC Financial Advisor Council.

Read coverage of CNBC's 2026 Financial Advisor 100 list, including stories featuring ranked firms and their advisors:

In open responses, several advisors wrote that they are taking a "wait-and-see" approach before changing their investment strategy with Nvidia and are telling their clients to do the same.

"Don't react to short-term headlines," wrote certified financial planner Peyton Jamison, president of Jamison Private Wealth Management in Alpharetta, Georgia. "The company's valuation is extremely attractive, and the focus should remain on its underlying fundamentals and long-term investment potential." His firm is ranked No. 78 on the 2026 CNBC Financial Advisor 100 list.

Asked what factors could change their approach to Nvidia, 13 respondents cited the outlook for AI demand, 11 pointed to valuation, and 10 mentioned company leadership or governance. Some said the decision depends on the client, with eight citing risk tolerance and seven mentioning portfolio concentration. Seven said they do not expect to change their approach.

Nvidia is scheduled to report its third-quarter fiscal 2027 financial results Tuesday, after the market close. In poll remarks, advisors said the company's fiscal third-quarter earnings will help determine their course of action.

Advisors said that for investors, what to do may depend on how much Nvidia stock they already own, when they expect to need the money and how comfortable they are with volatility.

"If this is a company that's going to keep you up at night and you just feel really uncomfortable with these unknown risks, then there was always the option to sell and move that capital to another stock or another portfolio," Sun said.

Nvidia exposure can also come through mutual funds and ETFs. Nvidia's large market value makes it a significant holding in many broad-market funds, including funds that track the S&P 500, as well as target-date funds in retirement-plan options.

Investors can check their Nvidia exposure by reviewing the fact sheets for the funds in their 401(k) or brokerage accounts, which typically list a fund's largest holdings. A stock's decline does not translate one-for-one into a fund's decline.

However, investors with dedicated technology, semiconductor or growth funds may have more Nvidia exposure than they realize, advisors say.

"Nvidia is a big part of virtually everyone's portfolio whether they take an active or passive index approach," wrote Andy Pratt, a partner and director of investment strategy at Burney Wealth Management in Reston, Virginia, which is ranked No. 34 on the 2026 CNBC Financial Advisor 100 list.

He said he advises clients not to overreact to short-term news and to let the dust settle before making decisions.

— CNBC's Stephanie Dhue contributed reporting to this article.

#2825. Must-See 65: The plan to kill the NBA back-to-back and re-energize the sport's biggest stars

It was well past midnight when the Philadelphia 76ers touched down in Charlotte. The star-studded team was in the middle of a back-to-back prepping for its league-high seventh game in just 12 days. You could call it an NBA "short night." Friday night in Philadelphia: a 7:30 p.m. ET national TV game against the defending champion New York Knicks. Shower, bus, shuffle onto the team plane for a nearly 500-mile trip down the coast. At the earliest, lights out at 2 a.m. ET. Then Saturday: an early 6 p.m. ET tip against the Hornets on Halloween.

Of the 426 back-to-backs on the NBA schedule, this was the second-shortest turnaround with travel: 22 1/2 hours, with an overnight flight in between.

Fans in Charlotte saw the cost. LeBron James and Joel Embiid jerseys filled the stands, but both James (sciatica) and Embiid (plantar fasciitis) were ruled out managing injuries on the second night of the back-to-back. Fans paid good money for tickets and TV packages, not to mention parking and concessions and merch, and expected to see them.

But don't blame the players. Blame the schedule.

In my decade-plus covering the NBA, I have written about back-to-backs many times before. But as the 76ers sideline reporter for NBC Sports Philadelphia this season, I hadn't experienced one quite like this. And it was only October. Hundreds more still litter the schedule. With injuries sweeping through the league's stars — Stephen Curry (oblique) is now out multiple weeks — and fan trust at stake, the fix is overdue:

Ban back-to-backs and move to a 65-game schedule.

The NBA has always sold its stars. Right now, the league is breaking that promise.

Anthony Edwards used to be a loud critic against load management. At his first All-Star appearance in 2023, the 21-year-old star responded to a question about the league's hot-button issue.

"Just play, man," Edwards said. "If you 80%, you gotta play. I don't like all the sitting, missing games stuff. These people might have enough money to come to one game. And that might be the game they come to and you sitting out."

Edwards walked the walk. Edwards played in nearly every game of that Timberwolves season, the first of three straight seasons playing 79 games. No All-Star played more than Edwards, per Stathead tracking, and the fans loved it.

Edwards was a box-office hit and seemed invincible, the face that could stem the tide of load management.

And then in March last season, his knee started barking. He looked limited against OKC in his 58th game and the medical team sidelined him. One pressing issue: He needed to play in seven of the 15 remaining games to qualify for postseason awards, which, until the OKC game, seemed easily attainable. His coach Chris Finch praised his Ironman courage and noted Edwards had played in 95% of his games in his career, "which is extremely rare."

But the game finally caught up to Edwards. He missed 11 of the team's next 14 games and hobbled through the worst postseason of his precocious career. He fell short of the 65 games needed for all-league awards and missed more time in the playoffs. The health problems have only continued. This season, after just four games, Edwards was shut down with issues in the same knee that he tried to play through last season. The team hopes he's back in two months after knee debridement surgery. This week, he was seen still on crutches. A league and its fans may wonder: Will he ever be the same?

Maybe Edwards saw this coming. The same week he was shut down, Adidas unveiled its latest commercial captioned "65 games one way or another" for its flagship player. The timing of its publication sliced like a knife. In the spot, Edwards, arguably the NBA's most marketable American star and once-crusader against load management, is seemingly surrendering to the reality of the 82-game grind. In the pre-taped ad, Edwards is holding auditions for his stand-in when he misses time. "If I gotta sit out this season, you gotta act like me, you gotta talk like me, most importantly you gotta hoop like me," Edwards cracked.

Edwards' body couldn't hold up and now his shoe company is mocking the whole thing. But it's no laughing matter for the players, fans and the league's business.

Injuries have dominated headlines in the early going. Less than 10 games into the season, Edwards was one of nine star players — as defined by the league's Player Participation Policy of having been named to at least one All-Star or All-NBA team in the previous three seasons — who have already suffered a significant injury. You can add Curry to this list.

The Oklahoma City Thunder, with dreams of being the league's next dynasty, have been decimated by the reality, with major injuries to stars Jalen Williams (broken fibula and dislocated ankle) and Chet Holmgren (knee), not to mention key player Cason Wallace, who is shelved indefinitely with a shoulder injury. Tyrese Haliburton, Jalen Duren and Kawhi Leonard are also injured on championship contenders.

The game is faster and more taxing than it was in previous generations. Steve Kerr told me during the 2025 playoffs that it's a young man's game, the sheer velocity making it tougher for vets in the playoffs. About a month after that, Haliburton tore his Achilles in Game 7 of the NBA Finals. Even the young players aren't immune to the harsh demands of the sport.

In 2023, when the NBA rolled out its Player Participation Policy, the NBA commissioned a 57-page study on load management. It didn't find evidence that load management affected injury rates in either direction. (Science is hard.) One chart, though, told a clear story that demands wholesale reform: The average star missed 10.4 games a season in the 1980s, growing gradually to 10.6 games a season in the 1990s, 13.9 in the 2000s and 17.4 in the 2010s. From 2019-20 through 2022-23, however, that number jumped to 23.9. Last season, I ran the numbers and it hit 27.7, more than two-and-a-half times the 1990s rate.

That's a lot of stars missing in action, and even more potential lifelong fans who are now looking elsewhere with their eyeballs and dollars. Fans waking up to find the news of another star getting hurt has become routine, even if, by my count, star injury rates are slightly down overall year over year. (Those Damian Lillard, Tyrese Haliburton, Kyrie Irving and Jayson Tatum DNPs last season added up quickly.) Even excluding last season's season-scrapping injuries, the overall trend line is unmistakable.

So, how do we get back to the pre-2000 DNP rates? Small tweaks haven't worked. A 40-minute game doesn't address the biggest problem: The sport is too taxing for so many games with crosscountry travel. A 65-game season that rejuvenates fan trust and interest is the answer. We need to invest and protect the stars that make the NBA what it is. We need to think about Felipe.

For the 76ers' back-to-back ending in Charlotte, what got me was seeing the kids in the stands, the ones who brought posters and chanted their favorite players' names only for the schedule to let them down.

This happens all too often. In 2023, an Argentinean boy named Felipe flew over 4,000 miles to see Jimmy Butler play in Miami. TNT cameras showed the devastation when the kid dropped his poster in shock when he learned Butler wasn't playing. Butler was a late scratch with lower back tightness. (Butler felt terrible and met with the family the next day with merch.)

The NBA has a real opportunity to further invest in delivering to families and younger fans who build core memories at games. Studies show that lifelong sports fandom is typically inherited by family ties and most were hooked by age 14. The Felipe story went viral and he got taken care of, but I worry that most just go home and think twice about buying tickets next time, turning on their Nintendo Switch instead of the big game.

When I think about improving an already great league, a 65-game season that eliminates these zero-rest nights is key. Why am I targeting back-to-backs? The link to injuries is murky, but it's the clearest way to get stars on the floor, and that's what keeps fans coming back. I've shown that star players suit up for more than 90% of Cup games, higher than the regular-season norm. Part of that is the stakes, but part of it, I suspect, is the schedule-makers keeping Cup games off dreaded back-to-backs. In the playoffs, where there are no back-to-backs, stars don't sit for rest and play almost every game.

James and Embiid join a host of stars who have already missed the second night of a back-to-back this season. Curry, Luka Dončić, Paul George, Pascal Siakam and Kyrie Irving have all sat a zero-rest game in 2026-27. This season, stars have played in 73.5% of games on the second night of a back-to-back, compared with nearly 80% of all other games.

The product is also better without back-to-backs. Victor Wembanyama's Wilt impression last Wednesday — 52 points, 24 rebounds and 8 blocks — came on a day of rest. That's no fluke. Wembanyama is dunking 40% more when he isn't playing on the second night of a back-to-back, continuing last season's trend where, according to Stathead tracking, he dunked 25% more with at least one day of recovery. League-wide, dunks go up when players' bodies aren't run down on back-to-backs.

So with all that considered, here's my proposal.

Every team plays 65 games in the regular season. I'm calling it Must-See 65. The league and the players union agreed that 65 games makes a star's season count. Let's make it real with a 65-game schedule and let's make it must-see TV.

Say goodbye to the 82-game schedule and bring back appointment viewing. Continue to market the stars and deliver more on that promise. Restore trust among fans and make advertisers more confident with their investments.

The most common rebuttal is money: Critics claim that owners and players will never agree to give up that much revenue. But baseball's owners just proposed cutting eight games without cutting player salaries. Owners are warming up to the idea that fewer games can mean more valuable ones. NBA owners have a possible cushion, too: Each stands to collect a reported $650 million or more in upcoming expansion fees.

For players, the upside is health. Fewer games and no back-to-backs mean fewer nights playing banged up, and potentially longer careers, which can be worth millions in future earnings.

With expansion on the horizon, the time is now. Ten months ago, I campaigned for a 58-game schedule as part of Adam Silver's New Year's Resolutions. But with expansion imminent, I wasn't thinking big enough. Two more teams unlock a new billion-dollar revenue stream in my Must-See 65 proposal.

Let's get into it. Here are some key points of Must-See 65 that will help keep revenues neutral:

• With the expected expansion to Seattle and Las Vegas, the Emirates NBA Cup turns into a 32-team single-elimination tournament midseason, seeded by record. Capture some of the magical dust of March Madness, a tournament that reels in more than $1 billion a year. (Before expansion, the top two teams receive a bye.)

• This year, the Emirates NBA Cup Championship Game will be played at Hinkle Fieldhouse in Indianapolis. Double down on that. Break the 32 teams into four regional sites in cities that have dreams of having an NBA team: Nashville, Louisville, Kansas City and Mexico City. You want an NBA team? Show us. With lots of money and fans, of course.

• Some schedule housekeeping: In the regular season, play each team twice — one home, one away — for 60 non-Cup regular-season games. With Seattle and Las Vegas added, each team skips one game against two non-conference opponents on an eight-year rotation basis. The Cup will take care of the remaining five games, with makeup games at regional sites for those who are eliminated. (Before expansion, the NBA can add two regular-season games to reach 65. Maybe package a rivalry miniseries of three intradivision games?)

• Here's the best part: The winner of the 32-team tournament gets to decide their playoff opponent until the conference finals. Yes, that means if you win the NBA Cup, you're guaranteed a playoff spot. The Opponent Selection Show becomes a sponsored TV revenue driver.

Banning back-to-backs could be the NBA's version of the pitch clock in baseball: a fix that fans can feel immediately. Baseball's attendance has grown in three straight years since it was added and the fan base got younger. Packaged the right way, a new season format without back-to-backs could lead to a similar bump in eyeballs and youth engagement. No more need to wait until game day to see if the star is ruled out. No more sad Felipes.

Will fewer games raise ticket prices? Maybe. But tickets are already expensive and star absences can hurt the fan experience. Fans will pay more for Hamilton tickets if Lin-Manuel Miranda is on the bill and they have a better chance of leaving happy.

The sleep-deprived Halloween game was notable for another reason. Fans in Charlotte saw a shorthanded Sixers team stumble its way through the game, eking out a victory in the closing minutes. Jaylen Brown was the only Sixer to dunk in the game.

Two days later, with a day off, the Sixers' star-studded lineup of Tyrese Maxey, VJ Edgecombe, Brown, James and Embiid returned to the floor. With more recovery for its stars, the team put on a show in a home win against the Bulls. The dunk count that night? Nine. That's the NBA that fans pay to see.

#2824. Lutnick faces questions over Nvidia spy scandal. His sons’ firm has deep ties to the chipmaker

In late September, Cantor Fitzgerald hosted Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang and other top executives from the chipmaker for investor meetings in New York, according to a Cantor research report reviewed by CNBC.

The Wall Street firm liked what it heard.

"We continue to view NVDA as the best risk/reward in our coverage," Cantor analyst C.J. Muse and his colleagues wrote in a research report dated Oct. 3 that maintained a $350 price target and overweight rating on the stock.

Yet the firm formerly run by Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick and now led by his sons, Brandon and Kyle, reported a nearly 50% decline in the number of Nvidia shares it held between September 2025 and March 2026, according to a CNBC analysis of securities filings that excludes options. Nvidia had been one of the largest stock positions reported in Cantor's filings.

The decline coincided with White House and U.S. intelligence officials pressing Lutnick to crack down on Megaspeed, a Singapore-based company suspected of funneling Nvidia's advanced artificial intelligence chips to China. Lutnick repeatedly resisted those efforts, according to Axios.

By the time Cantor hosted Huang in New York, Lutnick had also received a classified intelligence warning, according to Axios, about Huang's ties to a suspected Chinese spy and had appeared alongside the CEO at a G20 technology summit in North Carolina.

Even as Cantor pared back its Nvidia stake, it continued doing business across the chipmaker's AI empire. A CNBC review of securities filings, corporate announcements and Wall Street research found the firm has helped arrange billions of dollars in financing for Nvidia's business partners while maintaining close ties to the chipmaker's leadership.

Those ties have drawn fresh scrutiny following Huang's resignation Thursday and Axios' reporting that Lutnick repeatedly resisted a crackdown on companies suspected of smuggling Nvidia chips to China, despite President Donald Trump authorizing new export restrictions on those companies for a third time on Nov. 5.

An ethics expert said the relationship between Lutnick and his former firm's investments may raise questions, given what the secretary knew about Nvidia.

Richard Painter, the former chief White House ethics lawyer for President George W. Bush and now a corporate law professor at the University of Minnesota said that while "there's no proof of" Lutnick sharing nonpublic information with Cantor Fitzgerald, "the appearance doesn't look great."

"His conversations with his sons better be about publicly available information," Painter told CNBC.

There is no evidence that Lutnick's sons had advance knowledge of the intelligence concerns, that changes in Cantor's reported holdings were tied to any warnings or that the firm's business interests influenced their father's decisions.

"Cantor has to date from time to time had only immaterial stand-alone proprietary positions in Nvidia, unlike the positions reflected in 13F filings which were entered into in the ordinary course of business in connection with client swap transactions, that are common in institutional markets," a Cantor spokesperson told CNBC.

Cantor declined to answer CNBC's other questions. The Commerce Department, the White House, Howard Lutnick and his sons did not respond to CNBC's requests for comment or detailed questions about the findings.

As scrutiny of Huang intensified, Cantor Chairman Brandon Lutnick was publicly praising the chipmaker's leadership. "Nvidia is one of the great companies on the planet, and they have an amazing leadership team," Brandon Lutnick told Fox Business on Wednesday.

Trump is standing by Howard Lutnick. A White House official told Reuters on Thursday that the president maintains "complete confidence" in the commerce secretary.

But cracks in the administration's unity emerged Friday, when Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent told CNBC's "Squawk Box" that the Departments of Treasury, Defense and State and the Office of the U.S. Trade Representative all "were in favor of putting Megaspeed on the entity list for months."

Bessent pointed to Commerce as the department responsible for imposing the entity list, which identifies restricted businesses and other groups, adding, "You will have to ask them about their internal processes."

Nvidia was Cantor's second-largest reported stock position before Howard Lutnick left Wall Street to become commerce secretary in February 2025, behind only bitcoin investor MicroStrategy, now known as Strategy. At the end of 2024, the firm disclosed more than 3.5 million Nvidia shares worth $479 million, according to its quarterly securities filing.

That share count held steady through September 2025, but its value climbed 36% to $652 million, according to Cantor's quarterly securities filing, as the Trump administration moved over the summer to ease restrictions on Nvidia's H20 chip sales to China.

Over the following six months, however, the number of Nvidia shares Cantor reported holding fell by 49%, to 3 million in December and 1.78 million by the end of March 2026, with their value dropping from $652 million to $310 million, according to a CNBC analysis of securities filings.

Pressure on Howard Lutnick was also mounting on Capitol Hill. Sens. Jim Banks, R-Ind., and Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., in March urged the secretary to suspend or reconsider Nvidia's export licenses after three people linked to Nvidia customer Super Micro Computer were indicted on charges of conspiring to smuggle AI chips to China.

"The Trump administration, I think, leaks like a sieve when it comes to non-public government information getting to various hedge fund guys," Painter said, though he stressed there is no evidence Howard Lutnick had shared such information with his sons.

The filings do not establish why Cantor's reported share count fell or whether its overall financial exposure to Nvidia declined. Nvidia’s stock had more than doubled from its April 2025 lows by late October, before retreating in the following months. The stock fell about 6.5% between the end of September and the end of March.

Cantor's reported Nvidia holdings remained near that lower level through June 30, the latest quarter covered by public disclosures, at 1.81 million shares. Its next quarterly filing is due Monday.

Cantor has not publicly explained the decline.

Most of those shares were attributed to CF Secured, a Cantor brokerage subsidiary that uses stocks and derivatives to hedge customer transactions, according to its audited financial statements. Cantor also disclosed substantial Nvidia options positions during the time period CNBC examined, overwhelmingly skewed toward put options, which can protect against declines in the stock price.

As concerns about Huang escalated inside the Trump administration in May, according to Axios, Cantor was helping finance the expansion of one of Nvidia's newest business partners, securities filings show.

On May 7, Nvidia and Australia-based cloud computing provider IREN announced a five-year, $3.4 billion cloud computing contract and a strategic partnership giving the chipmaker the right to invest up to $2.1 billion in IREN.

"That's not a passive financial investment. Nvidia is a partner who wins as we deliver," IREN co-CEO Daniel Roberts told investors on the company's quarterly earnings call that day.

Five days later, as Trump headed to China aboard Air Force One, Secretary of State Marco Rubio warned him about Huang's relationship with a suspected Chinese spy, The New York Times reported on Tuesday. Huang, who had not initially been invited on the trip, joined the president's delegation during a refueling stop in Alaska.

Howard Lutnick had already been sent a highly classified intelligence memo detailing concerns about Huang's relationship, Axios reported.

On May 12, the day Trump departed for China, IREN announced the pricing of a convertible bond offering that ultimately raised $3 billion when it closed two days later. Cantor was one of seven bookrunners on the deal, alongside JPMorgan Chase and Goldman Sachs.

On the day the offering closed, May 14, Lutnick's Commerce Department quietly cleared roughly 10 Chinese companies, including Alibaba and ByteDance, to buy Nvidia's advanced H200 chips, though some of those approvals predated the intelligence memo.

It is unclear when IREN hired Cantor for the offering or how much the firm earned from the deal. Cantor declined to answer CNBC's questions about its work with IREN.

Still, Cantor reported a substantial position in IREN's stock.

By the end of June, Cantor reported $115 million in IREN shares and $362 million in Nvidia shares. Together, the two companies accounted for more than 9% of the value of Cantor's reported $5.2 billion in securities holdings, excluding options, according to CNBC's analysis of the filing.

Cantor didn't answer CNBC's questions about its work with IREN.

IREN was not the only company in Nvidia's orbit that turned to Cantor for financing.

In October 2025, Cantor advised AI infrastructure developer Lancium on a $600 million financing deal to support its data center campus in Abilene, Texas, the first site of Stargate, the massive AI infrastructure project backed by OpenAI, SoftBank and Oracle.

Newmark, a commercial real estate firm controlled by Cantor, also helped finance the Abilene campus, arranging a $7.1 billion construction loan in May 2025.

Ten months later, Nvidia agreed to invest $2 billion for a roughly 20% stake in Lancium, with up to $1 billion more tied to future milestones, The Information reported in August. Lancium, which is also backed by Blackstone, confirmed an investment and a technology partnership on Aug. 24 without disclosing financial terms.

It's unclear whether Cantor continued advising Lancium after the financing deal or played a role in Nvidia's investment. Neither Cantor nor Lancium responded to questions about the investment.

Cantor's reported ties also extend to Nvidia's supply chain.

In May 2025, days after Howard Lutnick announced the transfer of his ownership stake in Cantor to his children via trusts, Semafor reported that bankers at the firm had informally advised executives at Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co., which makes Nvidia's most advanced chips, on navigating Washington. Cantor called Semafor's report "baseless and false."

TSMC received billions of dollars in federal subsidies created during the Biden administration and administered by the Commerce Department to fund its U.S. manufacturing expansion.

Democratic senators called for an ethics investigation in December into whether the Lutnick family stood to benefit from his push to expand AI data centers.

And on Wednesday, Sens. Warren and Chris Coons, D-Del., demanded that Howard Lutnick, Rubio and White House chief of staff Stephen Miller preserve records on Nvidia's chip exports to China and intelligence warnings about Huang.

The senators questioned whether "prudent defensive counter-intelligence action was taken or national security recommendations were overruled."

"The place where I think the Trump administration has most failed here is in being persuaded by Jensen Huang and others to license the export of some advanced chips," Coons said Friday on CNBC's "Squawk Box," accusing the administration of turning a blind eye to the black market for more advanced chips.

#2823. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."

#2821. Retirees sometimes tap savings for one-off expenses. Here’s what advisors say to consider first

Retirees facing a large expense that's not in their regular household budget may turn to their traditional 401(k) or individual retirement account balances to cover it.

While retirement savings may be the easiest place to turn for the money, financial advisors say there are a few things worth considering before making one-off withdrawals.

"Be aware of the fact that it's not as simple as just taking out the amount of money that [covers] what you want it for," said certified financial planner Victoria Trumbower, managing member of Trumbower Financial Advisors in Bethesda, Maryland. The firm ranked No. 19 on CNBC's Financial Advisor 100 list for 2026.

Read coverage of CNBC's 2026 Financial Advisor 100 list, including stories featuring ranked firms and their advisors:

At the same time, research shows that many retirees aren't even spending their nest egg. About a third still have 100% or more of their initial assets when they reach their mid-80s, according to a May study by the Employee Benefit Research Institute, a nonpartisan research group.

"Retirement savings are meant to be spent," said CFP Alex Canellopoulos, director of wealth and a partner with Vista Capital Partners in Portland, Oregon. "A one-time withdrawal isn't necessarily a bad decision, as long as it fits within a sustainable retirement plan."

In 2025, about 15% of 401(k) owners ages 60 to 72 with balances of at least $100,000 who had left their employer made an ad hoc withdrawal from their account, according to Vanguard's How America Retires 2026 report, which analyzed more than 125,000 ex-plan participants.

This age group falls between the onset of penalty-free withdrawals — age 59½ — and the threshold for starting required minimum distributions — currently age 73. RMDs are amounts that retirees must withdraw annually from traditional 401(k)s and IRAs.

Not all 401(k) plans allow occasional withdrawals from ex-employees — about 43% did in 2025, up from 16% in 2015, the Vanguard study says. If a plan doesn't offer such ad hoc distributions, a former employee who wants to tap that money would have to withdraw or roll over the entire balance. Typically, they roll it over to an IRA and take withdrawals from there.

The most common reasons for retirees taking irregular distributions, based on a 2025 Vanguard survey of 8,000 clients noted in the recent report, are paying off debt, with 31% of respondents citing it; covering unforeseen expenses such as medical care and home repairs, noted by 23%; and discretionary spending, with 22% citing it as a reason.

Regardless of why you take the money, advisors say a key consideration is the impact on your tax situation.

Distributions from a traditional 401(k) or IRA are generally taxed as ordinary income. If you don't have other means to cover the tax bill, you may have to withdraw extra from your retirement account to compensate — which creates a tricky situation.

"It's like a double whammy because you have to pay income tax, but you don't have any money to pay income tax, so you've got to withdraw more money to pay the tax, and then you've got to pay taxes" on the extra amount, Trumbower said.

For illustration: If you need $50,000 after taxes and assume a combined federal and state income tax rate of 25%, the math says you would need to withdraw about $66,667. That's because the money withdrawn to cover the tax bill is itself taxable. A $66,667 withdrawal taxed at 25% would result in about $16,667 in taxes, leaving $50,000 after tax.

"It's going to cost you a lot more than you think," Trumbower said.

A taxable withdrawal from retirement savings can also trigger additional costs elsewhere.

For example, if you're receiving Social Security retirement benefits, the added income could cause a larger portion of that money to become taxable. Depending on how much other income you have, 0%, up to 50%, or up to 85% of your Social Security benefits may be subject to federal income tax. A large retirement-account withdrawal can increase that other income and make more of your benefits taxable.

For retirees on Medicare, a large taxable withdrawal also could increase their Part B and Part D premiums through the income-related monthly adjustment amount, or IRMAA.

For 2026, that surcharge applies when modified adjusted gross income exceeds $109,000 for an individual filer or $218,000 for a married couple filing jointly. Medicare generally bases the surcharge on tax information from two years earlier, meaning 2026 premiums are typically based on 2024 income.

"What catches many people off guard is that they often don't feel that impact until a couple of years after the withdrawal," said CFP Ciano Villaquiran, a partner with CURO Financial Planning in San Diego.

For some retirees, it may be worth delaying the additional withdrawal needed to cover the tax bill until the next calendar year, Trumbower said. That second distribution would be taxable in the following year, potentially spreading the income — and its tax impact — across two tax years.

"Pushing the [additional] distribution from the IRA into the following calendar year may possibly avoid jumping up into a higher tax bracket," Trumbower said.

However, she said, retirees should still withhold enough tax from the initial distribution to avoid an underpayment penalty. The federal income tax system is generally pay-as-you-go, meaning taxpayers must pay enough tax through withholding or estimated payments during the year rather than waiting until they file their return.

Generally, taxpayers can avoid an underpayment penalty if their withholding and estimated payments equal at least 90% of the current year's tax liability or 100% of the prior year's tax liability. For certain higher-income taxpayers, the prior-year threshold is 110%. Any remaining tax due generally can be paid when the return is filed.

If you have a brokerage account, that may be a better source to fund a one-off expense, Trumbower said.

Taxes on long-term capital gains — profits on investments such as stocks held for more than one year — are generally lower than ordinary income tax rates. Depending on your taxable income, some long-term capital gains may even be taxed at 0%.

For 2026, the 0% long-term capital gains rate applies to taxable income of up to $49,450 for single filers and $98,900 for married couples filing jointly. Above those thresholds, most long-term capital gains are taxed at 15%, with a 20% rate applying at higher income levels.

Depending on the expense, retirees may be able to borrow money instead of tapping their savings, Trumbower said.

For example, a home equity loan or line of credit could help pay for a major home improvement project, while a car purchase could be financed with an auto loan rather than a large withdrawal from retirement savings.

Of course, borrowing comes with its own costs and risks. The loan payments would need to fit within the retiree's budget, and the interest paid could outweigh the potential benefits of leaving retirement savings untouched.

Additionally, it's worth considering how the withdrawal could affect your savings over time.

"Pulling a large amount out for a one-time expense doesn't just reduce the account balance today; it can reduce what that money could have grown into down the road," Villaquiran said.

For illustration: $50,000 earning 5% annually would grow to about $81,400 after 10 years, according to the Securities and Exchange Commission's Investor.gov compound-interest calculator. If a retiree withdrew $10,000 at the start and left $40,000 invested under the same assumptions, it would grow to about $65,200 — a difference of roughly $16,300 after 10 years.

CNBC receives no compensation from placing financial advisory firms on our Financial Advisor 100 list. Additionally, a firm's or advisor's appearance in our ranking does not constitute an individual endorsement by CNBC of any firm or advisor.

#2818. The Rockets have reinvented their offense

The Houston Rockets have the third-best offense in the NBA.

Ostensibly speaking. We know that numbers can speak in half-truths. It's undeniable that the Rockets have been the third-most efficient offense at this stage of the season. Whether they're actually the third-best team in the NBA is something we won't know until later in the year.

Pedanticism aside, this team looks good. It looks different.

Let's start here: Alperen Sengun is having a career season.

Side note: I've grown tired of the discussion around "conserving energy" for one side of the floor or another. I know the modern game is different. It's faster-paced. Injuries have become more common, etc.

You need to play both sides. That's basketball. First it was Sengun, and now it's Amen Thompson. It's a flimsy excuse for poor play on either side. Unless you're Nikola Jokic or maybe Luka Doncic, you don't get to make up for one end on the other.

In 2026-27, Sengun is playing both sides of the ball. He's defending well, but more relevantly here, he's having a career-best playmaking season. Sengun is averaging 7.9 assists per game.

And I think that's directly causally linked to the team's improved approach.

Running actions through Sengun in the high post, out of dribble handoffs, and as a short roll passer just lends itself to motion offense. It's partly a numbers game: When you're getting assists from the five, you're getting more assists. More broadly, it leads to extra passes, unpredictability, and overall good NBA offense.

It's showing in the team-wide numbers. The Rockets are making 296.8 passes per game – 10th in the NBA. Last season, their 279.5 a night landed 23rd. That's a stylistic overhaul.

They're also reducing their isolation slightly, from 9.5% frequency last year to 8.1% this year. That's likely part of why Kevin Durant, as the team's best isolation scorer, has seen his load reduced.

It's not that Alperen Sengun has become a better offensive player than Kevin Durant. More so, this is a more appropriate alignment in terms of playing to each other's strengths. Sengun is a playmaker. Durant, at this stage in his career, should be a play finisher whose gravity is as important as his actual output.

A more cynical view may hold that the Rockets are just shooting better. So often, basketball reduces itself to a game of make or miss. So far, the Rockets have increased their three-point volume (34.3 from 31.5) and accuracy (37.4% from 36.4%). Plenty of Rockets – Alperen Sengun (47.6% on 2.1 attempts per game), Jabari Smith Jr. (42.1% on 6.3), and Marcus Smart (40.0%, 4.4) are having career seasons from beyond the arc.

For those of you getting deja vu, you're not alone. The Rockets came out of the gates last year with hot shooting, and then cooled off. That could happen again, but as long as they maintain their revamped approach to offense, they maximize the odds that their offense stays strong.

#2817. Falcons vs. Chiefs injury report: Zachariah Branch, Divine Deablo not practicing on Thursday

#2816. AI risks are the biggest threat to the financial system, Fed survey finds

Threats from artificial intelligence topped the list for greatest risks to the financial system, far exceeding any other dangers, according to respondents who participated in a Federal Reserve survey released Friday.

The central bank's twice-yearly look at a variety of dangers saw a surge in respondents saying that the biggest danger comes from AI along several fronts including debt, stock market valuations and security risks.

"Respondents noted three distinct areas of concern: (1) elevated equity valuations and the possibility that lower-than-expected earnings could result in lower valuations and a market correction; (2) cybersecurity risk, stemming from either a malicious attack by sophisticated state actors or damage caused by frontier AI models themselves; and (3) risks associated with complex forms of AI-related financing," the report stated.

The report comes with Wall Street on edge over high-flying AI hyperscalers, while communities are on alert for the rapid spread of data centers used to power the technology. Some 56% of voters in this month's midterm elections said data centers were of crucial or major importance in their choices, according to exit polling.

Respondents to the Fed survey released Friday include a variety of market participants, economists, analysts and institutional sources. The report covers the period up to Oct. 28, just a few days shy of the election.

Of the group, 87% cited AI as a "salient risk" to the system. That was up from 50% in the survey during the spring of this year and well ahead of the next most-cited danger, a risk asset correction, which was mentioned by 52% of those surveyed.

Other leading risks were higher long-term rates (48%), cyberattacks (43%) and fiscal debt sustainability (43%), the latter coming with the U.S. debt over $40 trillion and the budget deficit near $2 trillion.

The report otherwise found the system fairly stable, though it mentioned that hedge fund leverage is at its highest since 2013 and concentrated on a relatively small group.

#2814. The Lakers are starting to find their way

Simply looking at the Lakers 11-2 record tells you they've been playing really well to start the season. Irrespective of the competition, you simply don't win at this rate — including seven games in a row after Wednesday's victory in Milwaukee — without doing way more things right than wrong.

That said, one of the benefits of watching a team on a day-to-day basis is that you gain perspective on what you're watching, are better able to contextualize the results and can speak to the differences between, say, an 11-point win against the Bucks vs. a 10-point win against the Hawks against a six-point win over the Raptors.

The details from any one of those games matter and help provide the arc and narrative of where the team has been and, maybe more importantly, where they could be going. You can also find the themes that help you, even this early in the year, establish what the team actually is.

For example, the numbers tell you the Lakers are the best offense in the league and that Luka Dončić is on the sort of start to the season that forgest MVP campaigns. These things are related and are easily the top data point for why the Lakers have the record that they do.

Again, though, watching this team every day gives you access to more than what a visit to the NBA Stats page or Cleaning the Glass would tell you about the team. Those tools help contextualize what you watch, but they cannot replace what you observe with your own eyes. They also cannot prove the nuance of and aforementioned details that go into the progression — or in some cases, regression — that comprises the march through what is a marathon of an 82-game campaign.

Which leads me back to why I'm here in the first place.

While the Lakers are 11-2, I'd argue they are just now starting to find their way of what they are and who they can as a team. Because their record is what it is, I think it would be easy to conflate their success with them hitting the ground running, but that would be a mistake. It's not that the team isn't playing well — of course they are, look at their record! — but it's more that the version of well they're playing is just now beginning to become more well-rounded.

For example, when the Lakers' winning streak started seven games ago, Luka was in full destroyer of worlds mode, scoring 45, 42, 40, and 53 points in consecutive games. If we're being honest, the Lakers wouldn't have needed much beyond Luka playing to that level for them to win, but they were still getting complementary performances from Austin Reaves and Walker Kessler — both of whom scored in double digits in all those games too — as well as other role players stepping up on any given night.

Over the last three games, however, things have started to diversify more, with Luka and Austin taking turns sitting out in the Sunday and Monday back-to-back games in Detroit and Atlanta. Add in Quentin Grimes missing the contests against the Hawks and Bucks, and the rotation has been stress-tested with different players needing to absorb usage while stepping into larger roles.

And, with those opportunities for others to step up, we have seen the Lakers begin to better establish the team they can actually become rather than simply rely on Luka going nuclear while fueling the NBA's best offense.

Sandro Mamukelashvili's uptick in production the past two games is a perfect example of this. Mamu was not necessarily playing poorly before these past two games against the Hawks and Bucks, but he was most helping the team by being a connective passer whose ability to drive and attack closeouts was helping grease the wheels of the team's offense.

Against the Hawks and the Bucks, however, Mamu showed an uptick in offensive aggression, looking for his own shot more often and paying off those attempts with made baskets. After scoring a total of 16 points in wins over the Kings, Blazers, and Pistons, Mamu erupted for 23 points in both Atlanta and Milwaukee, bested by only Luka on both nights.

Following the win in Milwaukee, Mamu spoke about playing with more confidence and how it's taken time to grow into believing in himself to the same degree the coaches and organization have believed in him by signing and putting him into the role he's occupied.

Beyond Mamu, we've also seen a better play from Collin Sexton, both individually and in reestablishing his connection and chemistry with Walker Kessler. After missing all of training camp, Sexton has been up and down to begin the year, but the recent uptick in touches and playing as the lone lead guard on the court with Luka and Austin both missing games has not only given him more chances to lead the team's offensive attack, but his placement into more units where he's gotten to play with his former Jazz teammate, Kessler, has given him a familiar partner who he clearly has a feel for playing with.

In the last three games, the 42 minutes Sexton has played with Kessler is the most he's been paired with any teammate. While their plus-minus together is only plus-six in those minutes, when you consider Sexton's season-long plus-minus is a -22, this recent trend upwards is a huge shift from how his year began.

Much of this is fueled by them flashing an understanding of how to play off of each other, with Sexton seeking out and feeding Kessler for easy baskets on one end of the court and Kessler helping to insulate Sexton defensively by patrolling the paint and protecting the back line on the other.

When you add in that on any given night the team is also getting strong performances from Ziaire Williams offensively, well-rounded floor games from Jake LaRavia, defensive playmaking and shot making from Matisse Thybulle, hard-nosed defense and rebounding from Jarred Vanderbilt and mostly mistake-free minutes boosted by elite athleticism from Adou Thiero, the Lakers are looking more and more like a full team that is complementing each other instead of a one- or two-man show of Luka and Reaves that just so happens to be supported by a mostly anonymous group of role players.

To what degree the Lakers' record can keep up with their great start remains to be seen. I absolutely do not think nor believe they'll maintain an .800+ winning percentage all season. But that's sort of the point here.

The Lakers may not ultimately be as good as their record through 13 games, but they've also been winning without playing nearly their best basketball. They've only very recently started to resemble a group that understands how to play together and what to do in the various scenarios different opponents offer with their specific game plans tailored to their own personnel.

You can follow Darius on BlueSky at @forumbluegold and find more of his Lakers coverage on the Laker Film Room Podcast.

#2813. Nvidia stock suffers longest weekly losing streak since 2012 as market value sinks by $1.7 trillion

Nvidia investors turned bearish before this week's Jensen Huang saga. But the selling on Wall Street accelerated in the past few days, sending the stock to its sixth straight weekly drop, the longest losing streak since 2012.

The chip giant, which spent most of the year as the world's most valuable company, has lost 30% of its value during the losing streak. That's a drop of roughly $1.7 trillion in market cap and it pushed the company below Apple.

Huang, who co-founded Nvidia and led it for 33 years, resigned on Thursday after two separate extramarital affairs — one with an alleged Chinese spy and the other with an employee — were made public. The board named finance chief Colette Kress as interim CEO while it conducts a search for a full-time successor.

Nvidia shares fell 13% this week, the most since April 2025. News of the first reported affair hit the front pages over the weekend, when Huang sent a letter to employees apologizing for being "unfaithful to my wife." The board convened late Wednesday after The New York Times reported on the other affair, this one with the company's vice president of health care.

By early Thursday morning, Huang was out, capping a stunning fall for the Nvidia co-founder, who had emerged as one of President Donald Trump's top allies in the corporate world.

"With the unfortunate departure of CEO Jensen Huang we expect a challenging transition for NVIDIA over the next few months," Gil Luria, an analyst at D.A. Davidson, wrote in a report on Thursday. Luria lowered his price target to $200 from $300, but maintained his buy rating. "We believe the market has appropriately adjusted for this transition," he wrote.

Last week, prior to Huang's departure, Nvidia shares dropped about 9%. Much of the decline came after the midterm elections, which resulted in sweeping victories for Democrats and the passage of dozens of ballot measures that could slow the artificial intelligence buildout.

In the weeks before the election, Nvidia's stock was sliding alongside the rest of the AI trade on concerns that rising interest rates would punish debt-heavy companies. While Nvidia is wildly profitable, big customers like neoclouds are reliant on the debt markets.

Heading into next week, the pressure is on Kress. Nvidia is scheduled to report quarterly results on Tuesday. Analysts are projecting 91% year-over-year revenue growth to $108.9 billion, according to LSEG. That's up 18-fold from the same quarter in 2022, just before OpenAI launched ChatGPT and kicked off the generative AI era.

Analysts at Oppenheimer wrote in a report on Thursday that, even though they see the company reporting solid earnings, "we expect fundamentals to take a backseat near term."

"The departure of Mr. Huang could, in our view, translate to a lingering share overhang," they wrote.

#2812. Nations Championship: Fixtures and standings

France ended South Africa's 100% record in the Nations Championship with a sensational 21-19 victory over the world champions at the Stade de France on Friday.

Les Bleus have now won four of their five pool matches and extended their lead in the northern hemisphere standings to six points with one game to play.

South Africa remain top of the standings in the southern hemisphere but will be overtaken by New Zealand if the All Blacks beat Wales in Cardiff on Saturday.

England, who face Japan at Allianz Stadium on Saturday, are in second place on points difference in the northern pool after an emphatic 45-22 victory over Australia last weekend.

Ireland secured a 42-12 win over Argentina and are third, with Fiji the visitors in Dublin on Saturday.

Scotland are in fourth place with two wins and two defeats and face Australia at Murrayfield on Sunday.

Wales overcame Japan 28-21 in their first home match of the championship, but remain in fifth place.

France will face Argentina in the last round of pool matches on 21 November, while England will play New Zealand and Ireland host South Africa.

The finals weekend takes place from 27-29 November, with all six matches at Allianz Stadium.

Feyi-Waboso burns bright as England energy endorses Borthwick

Scotland's familiar frailties brutally exposed in All Blacks schooling

Why Wales have a lot to prove despite beating Japan

Ireland going from strength to strength - Murray

The Nations Championship pits the northern hemisphere teams who compete in the Six Nations - England, Ireland, Scotland, Wales, France and Italy - against southern hemisphere giants South Africa, New Zealand, Australia and Argentina, plus invited sides Fiji and Japan.

All teams will play each of the six in the opposing hemisphere once, with three rounds of fixtures staged in July and another three in November.

Their results will rank the teams within their own hemisphere, from one to six.

On the final weekend in November, there is a three-day play-off event staged at Allianz Stadium in Twickenham. The sixth-placed team in the northern hemisphere plays off against the equivalent in the southern hemisphere standings and so on, culminating in the two top-ranked sides taking each other on.

The winner of that final match is crowned the inaugural Nations Championship winner.

There is also a parallel, Ryder-Cup style hemisphere title. The winner of each of the matches on the play-offs weekend will earn one point for their hemisphere, except for the contest between the two top-ranked teams which delivers two.

The first hemisphere to earn four points over the weekend will be crowned winners.

The Nations Championship trophy and the shield awarded to the best hemisphere are pictured at the Tower of London in London [Getty Images]

Third-place North v Third-place South (20:10 GMT)

Fifth-place North v Fifth-place South (13:10 GMT)

Second-place North v Second-place South (16:40 GMT)

Fourth-place North v Fourth-place South (13:10 GMT)

First-place North v First-place South (16:40 GMT)

#2810. Microsoft apologizes for massive outage that took down Outlook and other 365 services

Microsoft issued an apology Friday for a wide-ranging outage that shut down Outlook and other Microsoft 365 products, as the company worked to restore service nearly 24 hours after connectivity problems began.

"We are deeply sorry for the disruption this incident has caused our customers and partners. We know the trust they place in our services and take that responsibility extremely seriously," a Microsoft spokesperson said Friday. "We are seeing broad recovery and stability across our services."

In a statement, Microsoft said the connectivity disruption stemmed from "an issue during an earlier network capacity upgrade." The software giant said the underlying cause of the outage had been addressed and that they were still working to restore services to some customers.

Engineers scrambled to solve connectivity problems that began shortly before 1 p.m. ET on Thursday.

Email and calendar service Outlook, along with multiple Microsoft 365 services such as Sharepoint and Copilot, were largely down during the morning hours in the U.S. on Friday and throughout the Asian and European business days. Numerous external services that connect with Microsoft products were also hit. Users began to regain access to email and other services Friday morning.

In an update around 10:30 a.m. ET, Microsoft noted "encouraging recovery progress" and said that "service availability and email delivery performance [was] improving across a growing portion of affected infrastructure" as Outlook and other Microsoft 365 tools began to come back online.

In an earlier update Friday morning, Microsoft said that email queues were "approaching normal levels," but that intermittent issues were expected as recovery efforts continue. It had previously given updates by region, with Asia Pacific services among the first to start normalizing.

Engineering teams have been taking affected systems offline for fixes and bringing them back online gradually stages. They are prioritizing efforts to preserve emails delayed during the recovery process and reduce email queues and backlogs, extending the timeout window, whereby delayed emails continue to attempt delivery, to 48 hours to help protect messages.

Microsoft says it has not detected any malicious activity causing the outage, which it initially blamed on a traffic surge in part of its infrastructure and issues with a component responsible for processing server health. The company said it would issue a full report once its assessment of the outage was complete.

The company said in its initial updates on Thursday that the connectivity problems related to infrastructure "elevated load across multiple regions," caused by a "number of servers being unexpectedly unavailable; straining the remaining infrastructure." Some servers were failing automated health checks, it said.

Microsoft shares traded largely flat Friday.

Governments and businesses across the U.S., Asia-Pacific and Europe said they were monitoring the situation, which was causing widespread disruption.

The U.K.'s National Health Service said the outage was affecting some of its services but that patients should attend appointments as normal. Calls "may be subject to long waits" as a result of disruption to the organization, a hospital group serving South West London and the English county of Surrey said on X.

Earnings for Swiss luxury goods giant Richemont were slightly delayed on Friday due to the outage. Electric truck maker Workhorse Group, which was expected to report third-quarter results on Nov. 12, rescheduled the release to Nov. 16, citing business disruptions caused by the global Microsoft outage.

U.S. Rep. Josh Gottheimer, D-N.J., told CNBC's "The Exchange" that he asked for a briefing on the outage. Gottheimer worked at Microsoft prior to serving in Congress.

"Is there something that went on that's nefarious here, or is this just something else? These outages occur in a lot of the companies, but the size of it and the length of it and the timing of it," Gottheimer said.

Air India said it had "temporarily rerouted" emails so it could continue to receive customer messages, adding that its flight operations were not affected.

Australian Home Affairs and Cyber Security Minister Tony Burke said in a press conference that Microsoft "believe they are dealing in some ways similar to what we saw with respect to CrowdStrike where there is an update that has gone awry," Reuters reported.

A fault with an update issued by cybersecurity company CrowdStrike caused one of the world's biggest IT system outages in 2024, hitting airlines, banks and more.

Microsoft reported in 2025 that Copilot, its collection of AI assistants and tools, had more than 100 million monthly active users.

The incident highlights a business "resilience and dependency issue that has been decades in the making," James Owen, managing partner of Digital Risks at consultancy Control Risks, said by phone.

"We have become, organizationally in the private sector and more broadly, increasingly dependent on a smaller segment of technology and infrastructure as critical to daily life and business activities."

"Organizations are becoming more aware through a geopolitical lens about what technologies they adopt, whether their services are provided by firms in the U.S., or China, or elsewhere, but this shows the importance of proper recovery planning in the case of an IT outage. That could be manual backups, protocols that do not require internet connection, investing in diverse suppliers and crisis management planning rehearsals," he said.

Amazon Web Services and Google users also reported outages on the site on Thursday.

Amazon wrote on X that services were "operating normally" and an "unrelated disruption elsewhere on the internet is prompting some inaccurate news reports." The company referred users to its health dashboard for "accurate data."

At 4 p.m. ET Thursday, Microsoft said it had "validated an alternative service health check method" that brought some affected servers back online.

"We're now applying this change more broadly," Microsoft wrote. "We're also preparing automated recovery systems to continually detect and restart unresponsive servers, to maintain service stability while this change progresses."

#2809. Nvidia is done with Jensen Huang. The fallout in Washington is just beginning

Jensen Huang is out at Nvidia. Washington is just getting started with him and the company he co-founded.

Huang, the leather-jacket-clad CEO of the world's most important chipmaker, resigned from Nvidia on Thursday after revelations that he had an affair with a suspected Chinese spy. But the spy saga is gripping Washington, and lawmakers are preparing for investigations into how the now-ex-CEO's affair may have damaged U.S. artificial intelligence dominance, the national security implications and the executive's close relationship with President Donald Trump and his Cabinet.

Republicans will have their chance first during their last six weeks controlling both chambers of Congress. GOP leaders immediately summoned Huang to Capitol Hill and said they would subpoena him if he doesn't agree to show up. In January, Democrats, who are already eager to make their mark with oversight when they take over the U.S. House and Senate, will be in the driver's seat.

"In this environment where there's some really high stakes when it comes to our national security, I would expect there would be some follow-up, yeah," Senate Majority Leader John Thune, R-S.D., told reporters at the Capitol on Thursday.

Huang and the woman with whom he reportedly had a relationship, Alice Huang, an executive at the Singaporean technology company Megaspeed and an alleged Chinese spy have drawn congressional attention. And Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick has drawn fire after reports this week that he stalled President Donald Trump's crackdowns on firms routing Nvidia chips to China.

Some Congress members have also called for an investigation into the role of the Bureau of Industry and Security, a subagency of the Commerce Department that manages and enforces export controls, in the Huang scandal.

The New York Times reported on Monday that Nvidia's general counsel, Tim Teter, claimed in emails to Commerce investigators probing Alice Huang's company that he'd been in touch with Jeffrey Kessler, who leads BIS. The Commerce investigation into Megaspeed, which allegedly smuggled restricted chips to China, was then transferred to the Justice Department.

By Thursday night, Senate banking committee Chair Tim Scott, R-S.C., summoned both Huang and Lutnick to testify before his committee in December. Later that same night, Lutnick had "proactively agreed to appear voluntarily before the Banking Committee to address their questions," Kristen Eichamer, a Commerce Department spokesperson, said in a statement.

Scott threatened to subpoena Huang if he does not appear. A banking panel spokesperson did not immediately respond Friday morning when asked if they'd heard from Huang.

"Recent reports raise serious concerns about our national security and the protection of sensitive U.S. technology," Scott wrote to Huang. "The American people deserve answers."

And with Democrats poised to take control of both chambers come January, the tech mogul's close ties to the White House will add fuel to an already burning fire as the new majority spools up myriad investigations into the Trump administration.

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., in a post to X on Thursday, said "Congress must demand answers from Mr. Huang and others at Nvidia about how they may have put U.S. national security at risk." Schumer is in a contested race to become Senate majority leader in the next Congress.

The senators who will lead those investigations, and hold subpoena power in the new Congress, are gearing up for Nvidia to take center stage.

Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., the likely incoming chair of the Senate banking panel, said in a statement Thursday that "Mr. Huang, NVIDIA executives, and NVIDIA's board of directors still owe Congress answers about the company's compliance with U.S. export controls

Warren and Sen. Chris Coons, D-Del., the top Democrat on the Senate Foreign Relations subpanel with jurisdiction over East Asia and the Pacific, sent a letter Wednesday to Lutnick, Secretary of State Marco Rubio and White House Chief of Staff Stephen Miller instructing them to preserve all documents and communications related to Nvidia's exports of "advanced AI chips to China or to entities that may divert such chips to China."

Even before Democrats take control in January, Huang is expected to appear before Congress.

House Foreign Affairs Committee Chair Brian Mast, R-Fla., is actively negotiating for Huang to testify before House committees and told reporters Thursday the Nvidia CEO has agreed to appear in the coming weeks.

Details of that testimony are still being worked out, Mast said. It would likely include his committee, as well as the House intelligence panel and the House Permanent Select Committee on China, and it would be under oath and transcribed. It's not clear at this point whether it would be public.

"We want to see him basically in a week … Sooner rather than later," Mast told reporters Thursday, adding that he believed it would happen before Thanksgiving.

If Huang doesn't willingly appear, Mast said he will subpoena him.

Mast, along with House Select China Committee Chair Rep. John Moolenaar, R-Mich., and House Intelligence Committee Chair Rick Crawford, R-Ark., send a second request Thursday to Huang and Teter requesting they appear and giving them an 8 p.m. ET Friday deadline to respond. Mast sent a second request to Kessler.

Rep. Josh Gottheimer, D-N.J., a member of the House Intelligence Committee, said Friday during an appearance on CNBC's "The Exchange" that he wants answers from Huang on the national security and export implications of the affair.

"From an intelligence committee perspective, you can imagine from an AI perspective, how important this is for our national security, for our critical infrastructure and understand when it comes to chip policy, what exactly got to China and ... did Megaspeed divert chips?" Gottheimer said.

The calls for immediate investigation into Huang and Nvidia on the day of his resignation represent a stunning fall from grace in the halls of power for the former CEO. Huang was one of the most effective voices for the AI industry in Washington — able to toggle between leveraging his relationship with Trump and defending his industry from fears about AI's risks to lawmakers.

"He is always the TED Talk guy," one senior Democratic staff member who was granted anonymity to describe private meetings they had attended with Huang, told CNBC. "He's really good and articulate, and really good at explaining why AI is important and why it's so important for the United States to be leading here."

The staffer said Huang was a "happy warrior" for AI, and stayed longer than scheduled or anticipated for questions from lawmakers. But in the meetings with lawmakers the aide attended, they said Huang did not aggressively push against export controls or get too "deep in the weeds" to really "lobby one way or the other" with the lawmakers.

Huang has repeatedly resisted export controls publicly, a move that has rubbed some lawmakers the wrong way.

Some lawmakers even lamented the loss of Huang, despite the inevitable buzzsaw he now faces on Capitol Hill.

"It's just sad all around," Sen. John Fetterman, D-Pa., said of Huang's resignation.

#2808. FAA grounds departing flights nationwide, citing air traffic communication system outage

The Federal Aviation Administration halted departing flights nationwide on Friday, a rare measure, after reporting communications issues affecting "multiple" air traffic control centers.

Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin said Friday at the White House that there was no indication that the issues were a threat to the United States.

The FAA said in post on X that there was a "temporary telecommunications outage" and that it halted flights out of an "abundance of caution."

The issues were related to communications and radar problems, according to an internal message from Alaska Airlines, which was seen by CNBC.

"The FAA issued a nationwide ground stop for all U.S. airports following communication issues impacting multiple Air Route Traffic Control Centers," the FAA said. These so-called centers guide airplanes at high altitudes along their routes.

Airlines said flights in the air would continue.

The FAA's technicians are investigating, the agency said.

A ground stop halts flights at their departure point. Departing traffic was halted until further notice, the agency said, disrupting hundreds of flights.

Disruptions tend to snowball if ground stops drag on as flight crews reach their maximum number of duty hours for a day.

Nationwide ground stops are extremely rare. In January 2023, the FAA halted flights because of issues with a pilot alert system. Before that, traffic was halted nationwide after the Sept. 11, 2001, terror attacks.

There were 11,000 flights scheduled to depart U.S. airports after 4 p.m. ET on Friday, according to flight-data firm Cirium.

Inbound international flights will be managed on a case by case basis, the FAA said.

United Airlines said it "will work with our customers to get them to their destinations once this issue is resolved."

— CNBC's Kevin Breuninger contributed to this article.
